Title: LLM Complexity: Towards a Computational Complexity View of Large Language Model Inference

  • Authors: Yu-Hung Wu, Shao-Xiang Yuan, Chan-Hung Yu, Zih-Ching Chen, Kuan-Hao Huang, Shao-Hua Sun
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24943
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24943
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.24943]] LLM Complexity: Towards a Computational Complexity View of Large Language Model Inference(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24943)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Computational complexity theory offers a principled framework for characterizing the efficiency of computational problems, and Large Language Models (LLMs) can similarly be viewed as computational systems executing a structured process of input, inference-time compute, and output. In this context, the number of generated tokens emerges as a natural and directly observable proxy for inference-time compute. However, we show that the tempting analogy between LLM token length and classical time complexity does not hold. We disprove the hypothesis that inference-time token usage aligns monotonically with classical time complexity, demonstrating a fundamental divergence grounded in how LLMs actually allocate computation during inference. To this end, we introduce LLM Complexity, a new metric designed to quantify problem complexity from the perspective of LLMs by characterizing the area bounded above by the accuracy-token curve. We find that diverse LLMs induce highly consistent task rankings under LLM Complexity, indicating that the metric captures stable notions of difficulty across models. We leverage this property to construct an easy-to-hard curriculum for supervised fine-tuning, which improves accuracy while reducing token consumption. Finally, we demonstrate that LLM Complexity generalizes to real-world tasks, producing more stable cross-model correlations than human-annotated difficulty labels.

Title: ICARE: cross-domain text classification with incremental class-aware representation and distillation learning

  • Authors: Son Duong, Truong X. Tran, Hai-Anh Tran
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24959
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24959
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.24959]] ICARE: cross-domain text classification with incremental class-aware representation and distillation learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24959)
  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: The rise of powerful, fine-tuned large language models (LLMs) necessitates robust natural language inference (NLI) systems. These NLI systems ideally train across various tasks, encompassing diverse concepts like sentiment analysis, news classification, and coreference resolution. However, this continual, multi-tasking approach can lead to catastrophic forgetting, where previously learned tasks are lost, especially in domains lacking specific labels. Rehearsing past examples with labels can further exacerbate this issue by causing overfitting, hindering the system's ability to classify new categories. To tackle these challenges, this research proposes ICARE, a novel framework with two key components: an instance selection module and a knowledge distillation method. The former is based on representation learning, which meticulously selects training examples to present to the NLI system. The latter, knowledge distillation with feature and logit-level transfer, facilitates knowledge transfer between tasks. This leverages pruning techniques and optimized objective functions to transfer both essential feature-level and decision-making (logit-level) knowledge, mitigating catastrophic forgetting while adapting to new patterns. Extensive evaluations on benchmark text classification datasets have demonstrated ICARE's effectiveness. The framework achieves a significant improvement in target accuracy and reduction in the ``forgetting metric''. These results solidify ICARE's ability to preserve and leverage learned knowledge across diverse tasks, ultimately enhancing the adaptability and performance of NLI systems. The source code is available at this https URL

Title: TETHER: Training-Free, Elastic, ToC-Anchored Hierarchy Extraction and Recovery in Design-Driven Long Documents

  • Authors: Yu Zou, Yan Chen, Jialei Zeng, Xiaorui Zhou, Aixi Zhong, Qi Zhou, Haoran You, Ke Jiang, Changyu Hu
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24960
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24960
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.24960]] TETHER: Training-Free, Elastic, ToC-Anchored Hierarchy Extraction and Recovery in Design-Driven Long Documents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24960)
  • Keywords: robust, extraction, large language model
  • Abstract: Converting complex documents into structured knowledge constitutes the critical infrastructure urgently required for deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) in downstream research. However, blind to the Table of Contents (ToC) as a macro-structure, existing bottom-up paradigms rely on localized visual cues, inducing heuristic fragility in high-entropy layouts where visual prominence decouples from logical depth; meanwhile, direct LLM extraction lacks spatial grounding, triggering hallucinations in long contexts. To address this, we introduce TETHER, a training-free framework that elevates the ToC from discarded noise to a vital structural prior, enabling robust adaptation across design-driven long documents. TETHER operates via a two-stage neuro-symbolic mechanism: first, it constructs a document skeleton from the raw ToC using a Large Multimodal Model (LMM) empowered by spatio-semantic constraints; second, it employs an anchor-based alignment strategy to tether content blocks to this skeleton, effectively resolving boundary ambiguities. Evaluated across a multilingual benchmark of ESG reports, product manuals, and annual reports, TETHER delivers highly precise, state-of-the-art (SOTA) zero-shot hierarchy recovery, outperforming deep hierarchical recovery models, dedicated parsers, and general-purpose LMMs. Moreover, stress tests highlight TETHER's resilience to sparse omissions and textual noise, demonstrating that its precise semantic alignment is fundamentally anchored in topological and depth fidelity. Crucially, ablations confirm the framework's architectural irreducibility, while achieving 90% token reduction and drastic speedups over end-to-end baselines, establishing a compelling equilibrium between structural precision and deployment scalability.

Title: Physics of Language Models: Part 4.2, Canon Layers at Scale where Synthetic Pretraining Resonates in Reality

  • Authors: Zeyuan Allen-Zhu
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24963
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24963
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.24963]] Physics of Language Models: Part 4.2, Canon Layers at Scale where Synthetic Pretraining Resonates in Reality(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24963)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Do standard benchmarks see the architectural differences that matter? We study it with 64 from-scratch pretraining runs of Transformers and three linear-model families at 1.3-8B parameters on 1-2T Nemotron-CC tokens, at a cost of roughly 100k USD per 8B run. In academic-scale pretraining (1.3B parameters, 100B tokens), architectural differences can all but disappear when the models are carefully tuned. Instead, at 1-2T tokens they become measurable: Canon layers, which add local token mixing, consistently improve Transformers, saving about 30% of pretraining tokens for the same MMLU accuracy at 8B. We also introduce GLA5 and GDN2, which improve gated linear attention and Gated DeltaNet with low-rank output gates. Surprisingly, with Canon, the simplest linear model, gated linear attention, matches Gated DeltaNet and outperforms Mamba2 even at 8B/1T. Therefore, the extra machinery of Gated DeltaNet and Mamba2 may do little more than mix neighboring tokens. Even at 8B/1T, however, high scores on standard benchmarks can hide weaknesses in basic in-context skills. In particular, linear models that match or beat Transformers on these benchmarks cannot, in fact, reliably retrieve information from a short 500-token context. Moreover, no model, Transformer or linear, reliably beats random guessing on arguably the simplest two-hop question, stated in a few short sentences, even at this scale. These findings $\textbf{challenge traditional architecture research}$ based primarily on standard benchmark scores. In contrast, small synthetic playgrounds, which predicted the architectural differences above at <100M parameters, can reveal what these benchmarks miss. For practitioners, one lesson is to include in-context reasoning examples in pretraining data from an early stage, rather than reserve them for later mid-training, let alone post-training.

Title: HireWatch: Evaluating LLM Compliance with U.S. Employment Law Under Contextual Pressure

  • Authors: Huy Nghiem, Maria Kostylew, Noam Kolt
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CY
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24970
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24970
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.24970]] HireWatch: Evaluating LLM Compliance with U.S. Employment Law Under Contextual Pressure(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24970)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: The deployment of large language models (LLMs) in hiring pipelines is growing, yet existing evaluations generally overlook their compliance with legal rules. To fill this gap, we introduce HireWatch, an evaluation that assesses whether LLMs comply with U.S. laws that govern the questions that job candidates may be asked. We construct a 200-item bank of lawful and unlawful interview questions, whose legal classification is based on U.S. federal statutes, regulations, and agency guidance. We instruct LLMs to select questions from this bank in preparing for job interviews, across a set of various occupations and contexts, and report the fraction of unlawful questions that LLMs select. Evaluation of 18 closed and open-weight LLMs across ~100,000 trials under different types of contextual pressure reveals that LLMs' legal compliance is brittle. While LLMs generally avoid selecting unlawful questions in low-pressure contexts, legal violations increase sharply under higher pressure. Explicit prompts to comply with law can improve compliance rates, but do not altogether eliminate legal violations. We publicly release our code and data at this https URL.

Title: The ROI of Small Examples: Optimizing In-Context Learning Under Token Budgets

  • Authors: Anxo Perez, Jorge Gabín, Javier Parapar
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24971
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24971
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.24971]] The ROI of Small Examples: Optimizing In-Context Learning Under Token Budgets(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24971)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) improves large language model performance at inference time without updating model parameters. However, these gains are not free: in practical deployments, longer prompts directly increase inference cost and latency. Under a fixed token budget, prompt construction therefore becomes a resource-allocation problem. While prior work has often focused on how many examples to include in ICL, we study the complementary question of whether the available budget is better spent on a few long demonstrations or on a larger number of shorter ones. We study this aspect in question answering (QA), by organizing candidate demonstrations into length-based bins and constructing prompts from examples within the same length range under fixed budgets. Experiments on two QA benchmarks (\textit{Beat the AI} and \textit{Natural Questions}), using six instruction-tuned LLMs, show that shorter demonstrations are generally the most effective use of prompt space. This effect is especially strong on \textit{Beat the AI}, and remains visible on NQ, although gains are smaller and saturate earlier in the long-context setting. An additional natural language inference experiment on ANLI R3 finds that one of the two shorter bins performs best for five of six models, although longer demonstrations remain competitive. Overall, our results highlight demonstration granularity as an important factor in prompt design.

Title: No-Harm-Jury A Jury-Based Multi-Dimensional Harm Evaluation Framework for Medical AI Responses

  • Authors: Antonio Puertas Gallardo, Mario Ceresa
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24972
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24972
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.24972]] No-Harm-Jury A Jury-Based Multi-Dimensional Harm Evaluation Framework for Medical AI Responses(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24972)
  • Keywords: privacy, robust
  • Abstract: We present No-Harm-Jury, a jury-based automated evaluation framework that scores AI-generated medical responses across seven conceptually distinct harm dimensions using a panel of five diverse language models as independent jurors. Responses generated by Ministral-14B on 3,000 questions drawn from PubMedQA, MedQA, and MedMCQA are scored by five jurors across dimensions of informational, social, psychological, autonomy, economic, privacy, and epistemic harm; the 151 empty responses (98 MedQA, 53 MedM- CQA) are excluded, leaving 2,849 valid samples. The median aggregation robustly suppresses individual juror biases while maintaining coverage through complementary specialisation. No existing system combines all five prop- erties of No-Harm-Jury: automated scalable evaluation, five diverse LLM jurors, robust me- dian aggregation (with a reliability-weighted extension), seven conceptually distinct harm dimensions, and application to medical QA benchmarks.

Title: CANDID: Candidate-Driven Intervention for Disciplined Execution in Local Computer-Use Agents

  • Authors: Woongkyu Lee, Jungwook Choi
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24977
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24977
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.24977]] CANDID: Candidate-Driven Intervention for Disciplined Execution in Local Computer-Use Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24977)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: We present CANDID (Candidate-Driven Intervention for Disciplined Execution), an execution-control framework designed to enhance the reliability of local computer-use agents (CUAs) in resource-constrained settings. While local CUAs feature robust baseline proficiency in single-step action prediction, they frequently fail at critical junctions due to rigid action loops or premature DONE decisions. Rather than imposing imprecise cognitive loads through textual reflection, CANDID repurposes parallel test-time scaling into a disciplined, execution-level intervention at sparse, failure-prone points. We introduce a loop-recovery mechanism to filter candidates away from repeated behaviors and a conservative, unanimous DONE gating policy that requires absolute consensus to terminate. Experiments on OSWorld demonstrate that CANDID's structural control significantly improves success rates, outperforming feedback-based alternatives with minimal execution overhead.

Title: Quantifying Cultural-Psychological Constructs in Four Centuries of Ottoman Court Registers

  • Authors: Sila Mutaf, Enes Yılandiloğlu, Mohammad Atari
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24981
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24981
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.24981]] Quantifying Cultural-Psychological Constructs in Four Centuries of Ottoman Court Registers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24981)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Populations vary along cultural dimensions such as individualism, collectivism, and honor. But how can these dimensions be studied in historical societies for which conventional psychological data are unavailable? Here, we introduce a computational text-analytic framework for quantifying psychological constructs in 54,670 Ottoman Turkish court records from 21 Istanbul courts spanning 1513-1911 CE. We develop sentence-embedding models adapted to Ottoman legal language and extend a theory-driven, text-analytic pipeline to capture cultural variation along the dimensions of individualism, collectivism, and honor in this historical corpus. We evaluate our models through multiple methods: document retrieval, semantic textual similarity, questionnaire-item classification, and validation against expert annotations. Our adapted sentence transformer (OTA-BERTurk) achieved a Recall@1 of .843 on the test set for document retrieval, a Mean Reciprocal Rank (MRR) of .806 for semantic textual similarity (meaning the best answer usually appeared as a top result), and classified questionnaire items with 89.58\% accuracy. Document-level performance varied across constructs and methods. Qualitative analysis further showed that high scores reflected historically specific legal and institutional patterns. We publicly release the models, construct representations, scoring pipeline, as well as an interactive web interface to support transparent, reproducible historical-psychological analyses of Ottoman Turkish court records spanning approximately four centuries. These computational tools enable the study of psychological variation across centuries of cultural and institutional change in a diverse empire.

Title: Ties at the Top: A Point-in-Time Comparison of CVSS and EPSS at a Fixed Budget

  • Authors: Gianluca Martinuz, Paolo Ceravolo
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24983
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24983
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.24983]] Ties at the Top: A Point-in-Time Comparison of CVSS and EPSS at a Fixed Budget(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24983)
  • Keywords: security, protect
  • Abstract: Organizations must decide which vulnerabilities warrant further analysis and protective action when the volume of published vulnerabilities exceeds their analytical capacity. We present a methodology for comparing vulnerability-prioritization strategies under an explicit resource constraint, represented by a fixed number of CVEs selected from a common eligible population. We apply this methodology to the Common Vulnerability Scoring System (CVSS) and the Exploit Prediction Scoring System (EPSS). For CVEs published in the National Vulnerability Database (NVD) from 17 March to 31 December 2025 and from 1 January to 31 March 2026 (Q1), we reconstruct each CVE's scores and status 30 days after NVD publication, using archived source data. Each cohort has one ranking per strategy at a 5% budget. Positives are CVEs added to the Known Exploited Vulnerabilities (KEV) catalog within 180 days after their decision day. The catalog is maintained by the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA). EPSS captures 16 of 40 positives in 2025, versus 5 for CVSS; alternative selections among tied CVEs cannot raise CVSS capture above 9. In Q1, CVSS captures 4 of 11 and EPSS 3, but tie-breaking can reverse this ordering. The score alone leaves most of each CVSS list undecided: the tie-breaking rule allocates 1,678 of 1,919 CVSS places in 2025 and 589 of 758 in Q1 among CVEs tied at score 9.8. Most chosen CVSS scores come from non-NVD providers; disabling those scores reduces CVSS capture from 5 to 1 in 2025 and from 4 to 0 in Q1. A fixed-budget comparison should therefore report its decision cutoff, eligible population, score provenance, and tie-breaking rule.

Title: What the model knows but does not say: monitoring medical LLMs through verbalized thoughts

  • Authors: Frederik Hauke, Jan Clusmann, Hanna Kreutzer, Christiane Kuhl, Tianyu Han, Felix Barajas Ordonez, Sven Nebelung, Jakob Nikolas Kather, Daniel Truhn
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24994
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.24994
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.24994]] What the model knows but does not say: monitoring medical LLMs through verbalized thoughts(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.24994)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: Efforts to keep clinical language models safe have concentrated on what enters and leaves the model, but a manipulated model can return an answer that looks entirely ordinary. Here we show that the model's own internal state, rendered in plain language, exposes manipulations the output conceals. Using a natural-language autoencoder, we translate the activations of a multimodal LLM (Gemma-3-27B) into text and let an input-blind judge read this verbalized internal state (the thoughts channel) and the model's answer separately. Across two clinical attack objectives (omitting a pathology, covertly recommending a drug), two input modalities, and two delivery mechanisms, the thoughts channel caught the injection significantly more often than the answer in all eight injected conditions (p<0.05), while both channels held near-zero false-positive rates on clean inputs; the omission result carried over to Llama-3.3-70B. The thoughts channel also registered unsafe clinical requests as harmful while the model was still reading them, before it decided whether to refuse. None of this requires retraining: an off-the-shelf verbalizer and a general-purpose judge are enough.

Title: Density Feedback Control for Transferability of Generative Sparse Adversarial Attacks

  • Authors: Dongsu Song, Jay Hoon Jung
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25001
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25001
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25001]] Density Feedback Control for Transferability of Generative Sparse Adversarial Attacks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25001)
  • Keywords: attack, robust, transformer, generative
  • Abstract: Generator-based sparse adversarial attacks train encoder--decoder networks to produce transferable sparse perturbations for black-box robustness evaluation. Their perturbation density, however, is set only indirectly by a fixed sparsity coefficient whose mapping to density is unknown and shifts during training, so reaching a target density requires re-tuning; this fixed pressure also yields little transferability gain once density reduction begins. We propose Density Feedback Control (DFC), which tightens or relaxes this coefficient at the end of each epoch according to the gap between the observed hard-mask density and the target, without changing the generator architecture or loss. At a 10\% target density, DFC brings the mean density of three base attacks within one percentage point of the target in a single run and improves the transfer attack success rate (ASR) of two of them on CNN and Transformer targets, including under per-image $\ell_0$ budgets, while perturbing fewer pixels. Tracing GPG's training, we observe that as DFC relaxes the coefficient, the transfer ASR recovers with the density and overtakes the fixed-coefficient baseline with fewer pixels. For EGS-TSSA, which restricts perturbations to source-model feature-importance regions, DFC yields no consistent gain; we analyze how this restriction interacts with density feedback.

Title: On the Impact of Feature Reduction Techniques on Adversarial Attacks

  • Authors: Mohammad Meymani, Roozbeh Razavi-Far
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25004
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25004
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25004]] On the Impact of Feature Reduction Techniques on Adversarial Attacks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25004)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, robust
  • Abstract: Adversarial attacks have posed significant threats to machine learning models by exploiting their inherent vulnerabilities. Researchers have established an interdisciplinary field called adversarial machine learning to explore these vulnerabilities and develop corresponding defense strategies. Feature reduction strategies are used to decrease the dimensionality of the data in order to reduce training time, avoid the curse of dimensionality, prevent overfitting, and mitigate redundant features. However, their effect on adversarial robustness across different classifiers and attack scenarios remains insufficiently explored. This study aims to analyze the effect of feature reduction techniques on the adversarial robustness of a diverse set of machine learning models under poisoning, backdoor, and universal adversarial perturbation attacks on the APT dataset. These models include support vector machine, decision tree, random forest, multi-layer perceptron, and k-nearest neighbors. The attained results are compared with scenarios where different feature reduction techniques are applied, including principal component analysis, permutation importance ranking, recursive feature elimination, and univariate mutual information. Metrics such as accuracy, F1-score, and mitigation score are employed to evaluate the effect of these techniques on adversarial robustness. The results demonstrate that permutation importance ranking and univariate mutual information generally provide the most consistent robustness improvements, while other methods exhibit more variable effects depending on the classifier and attack scenario.

Title: Attacks and Defenses in Decentralized Multi-Agent Systems with Hop-by-Hop Task Decomposition and Delegation

  • Authors: Matthew D. Laws, Maria Lazou, Alina Oprea, Cristina Nita-Rotaru
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.MA
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25056
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25056
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25056]] Attacks and Defenses in Decentralized Multi-Agent Systems with Hop-by-Hop Task Decomposition and Delegation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25056)
  • Keywords: security, defense, attack
  • Abstract: Decentralized multi-agent LLM systems enable independently administered agents, each with its own operator, model, memory, and configuration, to collaborate on complex tasks without centralized coordination. Agents autonomously decompose tasks, execute subtasks and delegate remaining work to peers, enabling scalability and eliminating single points of failure. However, decentralization also introduces significant security challenges, as no single entity has complete visibility into task execution or the authority to enforce correct behavior across agents. In this paper, we systematize threats from malicious agents in decentralized multi-agent systems and propose mitigation techniques. We first design and evaluate attacks against integrity, availability, and confidentiality, demonstrating that a single malicious agent can cause up to 100% task failure, inflate token consumption and runtime by up to 7.7x and 3.2x, respectively, and exfiltrate up to 94% of relevant prior tasks from benign agents. We then present a cryptographic protocol that produces an irrefutable verified execution trace of every action taken in the system and develop an auditor that analyzes these traces to detect and attribute malicious behavior. Evaluations on HiddenBench using gpt-6.1-sol and Qwen3.8-27B-FP8 show that our protocol incurs negligible latency overhead, while the auditor detects and correctly attributes 98% of attacks, including those that comply with the protocol.

Title: Infer3D: Inverse Generative Modeling Enables Robust 3D Reconstruction

  • Authors: Amani Kiruga, Mark Hamilton, Joshua B. Tenenbaum, William T. Freeman, Vincent Sitzmann, Yilun Du, Ayush Tewari
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG, cs.RO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25061
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25061

  • Keywords: robust, generative
  • Abstract: Humans can understand the 3D structure of scenes even from highly unfamiliar or extreme viewpoints. Cognitive science suggests this generalization stems from a top-down generative inference strategy. In contrast, state-of-the-art 3D reconstruction neural networks typically rely on amortized, feedforward inference. While fast, this pattern-matching approach degrades when faced with out-of-distribution (OOD) scene conditions unseen during training. Inspired by the generative nature of human vision, we propose Infer3D, an inverse generative modeling framework that recovers the latent variables of a generative model to best match the monocular input. To prevent degenerate flat outputs, we couple the generative image prior with a frozen 3D lifting network that constrains the optimization space to a valid, multi-view-consistent 3D manifold. Across a variety of controlled synthetic and real-world tasks, Infer3D demonstrates superior OOD reconstruction accuracy compared to feedforward networks trained on equal or larger datasets. Furthermore, mirroring how human perception requires more time to process confusing stimuli, we introduce an adaptive inference strategy that expends more test-time compute on ambiguous OOD examples while efficiently routing in-distribution inputs. Title: VisAlloc: Allocating Visual Supervision for On-Policy Self-Distillation

  • Authors: Haoran Xu, Tian Chen, Yuhe Wu, Yanbo Zhao, Junchi Liao, Hongyu Wang, Jiaze Li, Wentao Wang, Yanfeng Li, Rui Qian
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25069
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25069
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25069]] VisAlloc: Allocating Visual Supervision for On-Policy Self-Distillation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25069)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) offers a promising approach to improving visual reasoning in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, not all samples benefit equally from privileged visual guidance, and uniform token supervision can dilute visually grounded signals. We propose VisAlloc, a dual-level framework combining sample-level visual filtering with token-level counterfactual reweighting. A fixed paired-teacher filter retains samples answered correctly from an evidence-centered positive crop but incorrectly from a spatially displaced negative crop. At the token level, both EMA teachers score the same student-generated response, and their absolute log-probability differences measure view sensitivity. Additional supervision targets tokens admitted by sensitivity or teacher-distribution disagreement, with shared frozen-prior, template-bigram, and student--teacher divergence gates. Inverse-frequency redistribution preserves the total additional weight within each micro-batch, while the positive teacher remains the sole distillation target. Inference requires only the full-image student. Across eight benchmarks, our 4B and 9B models achieve macro-averages of 81.44 and 84.92, outperforming Vision-OPD reproductions by 0.97 and 0.52 points, respectively. Code on GitHub: this https URL

Title: FinVeriQA: Financial Research Agents for Question Answering: Routing, Retrieval, MCP Tool Use, and Provenance-Aware Evidence Verification

  • Authors: Meenakshi Rajpurohit, Anna Phan, Ravikumar Komandur Narayanan, Gunanidhi Ramakrishnan, Nathan Howland
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.IR, cs.MA
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25071
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25071
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25071]] FinVeriQA: Financial Research Agents for Question Answering: Routing, Retrieval, MCP Tool Use, and Provenance-Aware Evidence Verification(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25071)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) write fluent financial analyses but miscompute ratios, recall stale prices, and route questions to the wrong tool with high confidence. We ask which component should do each job in a financial question-answering assistant, and study it with FinVeriQA, an open-source multi-agent research prototype over SEC filings and live market data: a compact typed-decision model (Laya) routes each question and screens out advice requests, Python computes every number, retrieval and tools fetch evidence, Mistral-7B writes a cited answer, a code validator checks that cited IDs exist and that numbers match computed notes, and a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposes the pipeline. Verification must be independent of the evidence the writer saw.

Title: PriTabICL: Differentially Private Tabular In-Context Learning with Task-Aligned Cluster-Wise Synthesis

  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25073
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25073
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25073]] PriTabICL: Differentially Private Tabular In-Context Learning with Task-Aligned Cluster-Wise Synthesis(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25073)
  • Keywords: privacy, protect, generative, large language model
  • Abstract: Tabular in-context learning (TabICL) enables large language models (LLMs) to predict target records using a handful of labeled demonstrations in their prompts. However, these demonstrations often contain sensitive personal information. Differential privacy (DP) offers principled protection, but noise-based methods introduce substantial noise, distorting the feature-label relationships essential for accurate prediction, especially as data dimensionality grows. In this work, we study DP data synthesis for generating privacy-preserving TabICL demonstrations. We identify two limitations of directly applying general-purpose DP synthesis to entire datasets: modeling a global distribution can obscure local structure and minority patterns in heterogeneous data, while measuring broad attribute dependencies under a limited privacy budget can reduce the accuracy available for relationships most relevant to prediction. More broadly, global statistical fidelity may fail to preserve heterogeneous structures needed by downstream predictive and generative models. To address these challenges, we propose PriTabICL, a general framework compatible with existing DP synthesis algorithms. It partitions records into DP clusters and synthesizes each independently, using a fixed feature-target workload to prioritize prediction-relevant statistical relationships. At inference, each query is routed to a DP cluster, and demonstrations are sampled from its synthetic dataset without additional privacy loss for the original records. Experiments on four real-world datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of our methods across heterogeneous datasets. In particular, one of our instantiations, PriTabICL-PrivSyn, improves over global PrivSyn in 8 out of 12 settings and outperforms existing private TabICL approaches in nearly all evaluated settings. Open-source code is available.

Title: HapBO: Harnessed $α$-$π$ Bayesian Optimization via Expert Natural Language Priors

  • Authors: Jean-Sébastien Giroux, Rose Guay Hottin, Marco Bonizzato
  • Subjects: cs.LG, stat.ML
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25100
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25100
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25100]] HapBO: Harnessed $α$-$π$ Bayesian Optimization via Expert Natural Language Priors(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25100)
  • Keywords: robust, large language model
  • Abstract: Bayesian Optimization (BO) can be substantially more sample-efficient when informed by prior knowledge about the problem. In practice, however, such knowledge is often available only in natural language, e.g., expert intuitions or documentation, rather than as the explicit probability distribution landscape that prior-guided BO methods may require. Leveraging it effectively demands both an expressive mapping from language to priors and robustness to information that may be misleading. We propose HapBO (Harnessed $\alpha$-$\pi$ Bayesian Optimization), which elicits beliefs once from a human expert, a large language model (LLM), or both, and deterministically compiles a structured specification into a reusable belief function ($\pi: \mathcal{X} \to[0,1]$), with a rich representation not restricted to a Gaussian family. Building on $\alpha$-$\pi$BO, HapBO applies this belief function through a bounded positive multiplier on any nonnegative acquisition, preserving positive acquisition support and requiring no LLM calls during optimization. We prove that, for the Expected Improvement acquisition function (EI), HapBO preserves the standard simple-regret rate up to a bounded factor, even under adversarial beliefs. We evaluate HapBO on 40 HPOBench tasks, a synthetic suite in 6-15 dimensions and two real-world applications: cortical neurostimulation and constrained solar design. HapBO achieves the lowest aggregate HPOBench regret and strong real-world task performance, with zero in-loop token cost. In real-world applications it improves the optimization outcome even at budgets of 12-20 queries, and at a small fraction of the wall-clock and token costs of other LLM-based methods.

  • Keywords: attack, robust
  • Abstract: Face presentation attack detection (PAD) systems deployed in border control and airport surveillance are constrained by a critical assumption: subjects are captured at close range, typically under one meter. No existing benchmark systematically addresses multi-distance capture, diverse environmental conditions, and concurrent spoofing and occlusion-based attacks, leaving deployed models underprepared for real-world scenarios. We introduce a video-based PAD dataset of 374,000+ frames from 261 unique identities collected across five standoff distances (1 m-100 m) under indoor and outdoor conditions using a DSLR camera (C) and a mobile sensor (M), with three-class labels: live, spoof, and props (occlusions); models are trained and evaluated on video clips rather than single frames. These results establish capture distance and sensor resolution as critical but underexplored axes of PAD difficulty. The dataset will be publicly released under an End-User License Agreement to support reproducible long-range PAD research.

  • Abstract: Localizing object boundaries is important for embodied visual systems, since small boundary errors can change the inferred geometry of physical surfaces.   • Abstract: Long-horizon LLM agents often exceed their context windows, forcing them to discard information that may be needed later. Our results show that a plain-text log and grep provide a simple but competitive approach to long-horizon agent memory, without requiring complex structured memory infrastructure.

  • Abstract: Security protocol analysis has emphasized message-passing, as this is how different principals interact in authentication, key exchange, and similar functionalities. Title: Separating Interpretability and Reconstruction in Sparse Autoencoders

  • Authors: Anh Tien Nguyen, Dimitris Samaras, Mahdi S. Hosseini
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25165
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25165
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25165]] Separating Interpretability and Reconstruction in Sparse Autoencoders(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25165)
  • Keywords: interpretability
  • Abstract: Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) are effective tools for mechanistic interpretability by decomposing neural representations into sparse and interpretable features. However, a single sparse representation in traditional SAEs is responsible for both reconstruction fidelity and interpretability, which are not guaranteed to align. To separate these objectives, we propose DualSAE, a modular framework that augments standard SAEs with a plug-in Residual Branch. A standard SAE serves as the Sparse Branch, learning a sparse code that contains the dominant semantics of the input. The added Residual Branch models the non-semantic information omitted by the Sparse Branch. We ensure that the contributions of the two branches towards the overall reconstruction have minimal overlap through a decorrelation loss. We also adopt a two-stage training strategy that first stabilizes sparse representation learning before coupling with the Residual Branch. Across diverse encoder-SAE configurations on two vision datasets and two language corpora, DualSAE achieves mean relative improvements of approximately 29% in reconstruction fidelity and 34% in interpretability (measured by monosemanticity score). Moreover, with more interpretable representations, DualSAE yields consistent performance gains in the downstream applications of topic modeling, hypothesis generation, and LLM steering. Qualitative analysis further suggests that the Sparse Branch indeed preserves dominant semantic content, whereas the Residual Branch captures non-semantic information to improve reconstruction fidelity.

  • Abstract: Human-object motion synthesis requires coordinated human participation as objects move toward specified goals. We represent intended participation through hand-part relations that specify the participating hand, its part-relative reference position, and participation interval.   • Abstract: Can the allocation used to train experts also predict which expert will perform best on a prompt? Players choose positions to maximize their expected shares of the prompt distribution, without using expert losses. Because the router is fixed, we can trace harmful behavior to a single expert and retrain only that expert, which lowers HarmBench attack success from 0.51 to 0.02 while keeping instruction following. The game also gives us several controls over how the experts specialize, including the kernel choice and its parameters, the trait-space geometry, and the number of experts. Importantly, InflEns is a general framework that can be extended to any encoder and data distribution.

  • Subjects: cs.CV

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25204

  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Whole-body human recognition has advanced rapidly with large vision models. However, existing methods remain fragile under real-world variations, as they tie identity to transient cues such as clothing, texture, pose, or viewpoint. We argue that a robust recognizer should not merely learn to ignore these cues, but imagine how a person would be represented if they changed. To this end, we propose IDWorld, an identity world model: just as a latent world model predicts the features of a scene after an action, IDWorld predicts how a person would be represented under a changed condition. In a single forward pass, IDWorld imagines these counterfactual features under shared canonical conditions, such as the same outfit, a fixed view, or a bare silhouette, without rendering any pixels. Compared with explicit imagination in pixels, this latent imagination keeps nearly the same accuracy while being over 11x faster, at 5.9 ms per frame. Since each imagined condition has its own strengths, IDWorld then fuses them adaptively, weighting each condition for every probe-gallery pair. Extensive experiments show that IDWorld achieves state-of-the-art results on CCPG and CCGR-MINI, outperforms the compared methods on MEVID, and generalizes best to CASIA-B* and SUSTech1K, with the best mAP on CCVID. Title: Reject and Report: Forgery-Evident Undetectable Watermarks for Generative Image Models

  • Authors: Yihan Wang, Haochen Sun, Xi He, Yaoliang Yu, N. Asokan
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25226
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25226
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25226]] Reject and Report: Forgery-Evident Undetectable Watermarks for Generative Image Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25226)
  • Keywords: robust, watermark, generative
  • Abstract: Provenance schemes such as those based on content watermarks let images from a generative model be traced back to it. A practical watermarking scheme must be accurate at a low false-positive rate (FPR), robust under content-preserving processing, and undetectable. In addition, it must resist forgery; otherwise, even from a single watermarked image and a surrogate model, an adversary can imprint the watermark onto an arbitrary image or regenerate new content that carries it, falsely attributing harmful content to a model or making authentic photographs appear AI-generated. We argue that a watermarking scheme should not only reject forgeries but also report them: a forgery rejected as unwatermarked leaves no trace of the attempt. State-of-the-art forgery-resilient undetectable watermarking schemes, such as SemBind, fall short on both counts. External anomaly detectors that do report forgeries either miss most of them or falsely flag genuine images after common processing. We propose Tripwire-WM, the first forgery-evident framework for undetectable watermarks. It commits the semantics of the generated image to the magnitudes of the initial latent noise, which the sign-based watermarks leave unused, through a distribution-preserving embedding; a forged image carries a valid watermark but with a commitment that does not match its content, so the detector both rejects it and reports it as forged. Built on the pseudorandom code (PRC) watermark and Gaussian Shading++, Tripwire-WM provably preserves their undetectability. On MS-COCO, with the owner's own model as the surrogate, it accepts no imprinting forgery, compared with up to 55.0% for SemBind, and reports all of them as forged; it also accepts at most 1.8% of reprompting forgeries, against up to 46.2% for SemBind. Meanwhile, it accepts at least 99% of genuine watermarked images at 10^{-6} FPR and matches the robustness of the base schemes.

Title: Some Memories Must Fade: Persistence and Decay of User Memories in Model Parameters

  • Authors: Sanidhya Vijayvargiya, Rahul Lokesh
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25235
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25235
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25235]] Some Memories Must Fade: Persistence and Decay of User Memories in Model Parameters(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25235)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Parametric memory allows Large Language Models to internalize knowledge directly into their weights, offering a potentially powerful alternative to retrieval-based memory (RAG). However, existing approaches to parametric memory are difficult to deploy as they largely mirror the Continual Learning (CL) objective of retaining information indefinitely. This is neither sustainable nor sufficient for a memory system, which must govern knowledge across its lifecycle by retaining stable facts, reinforcing recurring observations, updating changed beliefs, and allowing transient information to fade. We propose a system for parametric memory management consisting of a memory substrate (handling information storage and forgetting) and a learned policy (what to remember, how deeply, and for how long). Our substrate is a fixed-capacity LoRA adapter that consists of k-hot rank combinations which store facts without catastrophic interference and maintain an independently controlled temporal decay, enabling outdated information to fade while long-term facts persist. On this substrate, we train a lightweight controller that governs each incoming user event to ensure the memory system reflects the user's true, updated belief state. To evaluate how memory systems manage user memory, we introduce a controlled dataset of long-horizon user trajectories with varying persistence of facts based on changing belief states and user-behavioral shifts, and probe queries that test recall while information is valid and abstention after it expires. Our policy learns to effectively manage user memory over its entire lifecycle, balancing recall and abstention to achieve 86% of oracle performance, unlike CL and RAG baselines that primarily default to one of the two behaviors. By providing the first system to manage memory directly in model weights, this work establishes a foundation for long-horizon parametric memory.

  • Abstract: Reliable visual perception under degradation requires distinguishing task-relevant content from degradation-induced variations, especially in concealed object segmentation (COS), where foreground cues are inherently weak. The central challenge is not merely improving visibility, but identifying reliable content evidence amid degradation-induced variations. RGB-space segmentation often entangles content and degradation cues, while restoration-based methods primarily focus on image recovery and task interaction. This motivates explicit factorization to distinguish content evidence from degradation effects and assess its reliability. Retinex provides a spatially aligned basis for this separation but remains illumination-centric. We therefore extend it to content-degradation formation with task-asymmetric factor roles. The Factor Unfolding Network (FUN) uses the content factor R as the sole source of direct semantic evidence for segmentation, while the degradation factor L captures degradation-related variations and generates a spatial reliability map to calibrate this evidence. A unified energy couples observation formation with reliability-weighted foreground-background separation, and its alternating updates yield a coarse-to-fine network without clean-image supervision. In this way, FUN factorizes degradation for perception rather than removing it. Extensive experiments across clean and degraded conditions and diverse segmentation tasks demonstrate robust performance and generalization. Code will be released.

Title: Moving Generality from Model to Modeler: LLM Agents for PHM on a Deployment-Style Benchmark

  • Authors: Zhenling Chen, Zhiguo Zeng, Anne Barros
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25252
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25252
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25252]] Moving Generality from Model to Modeler: LLM Agents for PHM on a Deployment-Style Benchmark(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25252)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Coding agents built on large language models (LLMs) now help develop many models published as general solutions. Should generality instead reside in the agent that builds a model for each new task? We examine this question in prognostics and health management (PHM), where every model must work on unseen equipment. Our deployment-style benchmark, NextUnit-Bench, covers 24 public datasets split into 58 folds that hold out units or operating conditions; each agent has two hours to deliver an executable predictor, then run on the hidden units. First, frontier agents outperform AutoML, which reaches less than half the best agent's score, and faithful implementations of published methods on four datasets, of which only 6 of 30 beat the strongest agent's own design; published accuracies, where reproducible, rest on the papers' own conventions, and a single convention, capping the remaining life of test engines, can halve the reported error. Second, the agents' advantage is a design made on each machine's data; once made, a plain-text plan of a few hundred words carries most of the gap between two frontier agents, and given a weaker agent's plan, the strongest agent falls to that agent's level, often to identical predictions. Before touching the data, however, it matters little whose plan an agent carries out, and domain toolkits appear to help neither: what sets the strongest apart is how much it learns from the data. Third, such designs reach only implementers that can execute them: given the strongest agent's designs, a locally deployable open-weight model delivers half as often, even when they are lightened, and scores no higher than without them. Generality, we argue, is not disappearing but moving, from the model to the modeler.

Title: Process-Knowledge-Guided Latent Diffusion for Generative Design of Porous Architectures: A Case Study in Aerosol Jet Printing

  • Authors: Ngoc Dang Khoa Nguyen, Hyunwoong Ko
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25264
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25264
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25264]] Process-Knowledge-Guided Latent Diffusion for Generative Design of Porous Architectures: A Case Study in Aerosol Jet Printing(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25264)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: Printed porous layouts interleave functional material with open channels, and the layout that uses the material best depends on how the process settings shape the deposit. Generative models propose layouts quickly, but a generator that does not receive the process setting samples from the same distribution at every setting, even when the process-dependent score ranks candidate layouts differently. This paper examines whether process knowledge can steer a latent diffusion generator toward layouts suited to a given process setting in a prescribed design domain. We formulate a process-dependent utilization score in which a process model sets the length over which the utility of printed material decays with distance from a channel. The generator combines a vector-quantized variational autoencoder (VQ-VAE) latent representation, a topology U-Net, a ControlNet domain branch, and a separately trained regressor that predicts the score from noised latents; the domain branch conditions each denoising step and the regressor gradient steers it. The method is applied to aerosol jet printing, where the atomizer and sheath gas flows enter the score through the flow-focusing relation. Across seven modeled flow settings, the lowest-scoring candidate layout of a domain differed between settings in 191 of 500 domains. In a sampling study, the mean directly computed score on three validation domains and one test domain was 0.642 with domain conditioning alone, 0.636 at a weight of 300, and 0.450 at 12,000 (112 scores per weight; lower is better). The results support process-knowledge-guided generation as an approach to process-specific porous design under the stated assumptions.

Title: Real Cameras are Radiometric

  • Authors: Ties Harder, Felix Seegräber, David Nakath, Hendrik Sauer, Kevin Köser, James Pritts
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25265
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25265
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25265]] Real Cameras are Radiometric(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25265)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: We propose the radiometric camera, a model that lifts any central geometric camera model to a radiometric one. The lifted model keeps the projection of the geometric model and adds a finite aperture, a free-form focus surface and a generic vignetting function. We calibrate it by inverse rendering directly on the relative irradiance of the sensor, from the same images of standard calibration targets used in geometric calibration. Geometric calibration absorbs the bias of its feature detections. The proposed radiometric calibration uses no features, and our radiometric cameras have lower reprojection errors on held-out test sets. The focus surface extends defocus modeling from the thin lens to any central camera, and we use it in a first multi-view radiometric reconstruction, which recovers the in-focus reflectance of a texture from defocused images. The vignetting is estimated jointly and accurately reproduces a flat field measured through a diffuser. Because every standard camera model lifts to a radiometric one, the radiometric camera naturally and directly extends generative scene models beyond multi-view geometry to multi-view radiometric ones, where phenomena such as lighting and attenuation can be accurately estimated.

Title: Learning to Verify: Signature-Guided Training for Adaptive Adversarial Detection

  • Authors: Arash Vashagh, Roozbeh Razavi-Far
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25271
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25271
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25271]] Learning to Verify: Signature-Guided Training for Adaptive Adversarial Detection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25271)
  • Keywords: attack, robust
  • Abstract: Adversarial example detectors often fail when attackers explicitly attempt to bypass them. We propose a dual-model approach that keeps the original classifier fixed and trains a separate verifier to assess its predictions. During training, we identify patterns of internal neural network activations that distinguish genuine examples of each class from adversarial examples targeting that class. These patterns, called activation signatures, guide the generation of challenging adversarial examples that mislead the classifier, challenge the verifier, and internally resemble the target class. The verifier learns to recognize these examples while preserving its performance on clean inputs through knowledge distillation. At inference, the verifier evaluates the classifier's prediction without requiring activation signatures. We evaluate the approach on publicly available datasets against several adaptive attacks, including attacks unseen during training. The proposed verifier substantially outperforms directly attacked confidence-based and feature-based detectors under the evaluated L-infinity attacks. Ablation studies show that adaptive verifier training is the main source of this improvement, while signature guidance primarily benefits clean accuracy. Additional L2 evaluations reveal limitations in robustness across perturbation types.

Title: LLMs Are Already Confidence-Aware Zero-Shot Classifiers

  • Authors: Owen Cook, Xingyi Song
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25289
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25289
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25289]] LLMs Are Already Confidence-Aware Zero-Shot Classifiers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25289)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Zero-shot large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as text classifiers, but their labels are only useful when we know to what extent we can trust them. We compare black-box methods (verbalised confidence and sampling) with white-box methods that read token probabilities (P(True), and multiple logit-scoring methods using constrained generation). We evaluate eleven open-weight LLMs (1B-32B parameters) on five classification datasets with 3 to 151 labels, measuring classification performance (accuracy, F1-macro), discrimination (AUROC), and calibration (ECE and Brier score). We also introduce decision-only scoring, which only scores positions where labels diverge in the prefix tree (trie). It reduces decode steps by 88-96% on the three largest label sets and performs comparably to full-label scoring. The best open-weight model-method configurations (Gemma 4 31B and Qwen 3.5 9B) are competitive with the commercial Jev 1.13.0 classifier.

Title: Cross-Question Reliable Reinforcement Learning

  • Authors: Hector G. Rodriguez, Marcus Rohrbach
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25292
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25292
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25292]] Cross-Question Reliable Reinforcement Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25292)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: AI systems operating near the limits of their capabilities can maximize utility while minimizing the risk of error by abstaining or deferring questions they are not confident about to a human or a stronger model. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have been post-trained to estimate confidence in their own answers. To achieve this, methods reinforce confidence scores based on the correctness signal to each question-answer pair individually, with rewards that encourage assigning confidence 1 to correct answers and 0 to incorrect answers. However, estimating confidence scores is useful in that it enables selecting only the most confident answers, to maximize the share of solved questions while accommodating the user's risk tolerance. Assigning confidence 1 to correct answers and 0 to incorrect ones is theoretically optimal, but unattainable in practice and detrimental for selective prediction with a non-zero risk tolerance: confidences pushed to 0 and 1 carry no information about how answers compare to one another. We therefore introduce CARE (Cross-question Abstention Reward Estimation), which computes rewards over a set of question-answer pairs: At each of several risk tolerances, the confidences across the set induce an abstention threshold, and each confidence is rewarded for lying on the correct side of it. Since the threshold itself comes from the set, every verbalized confidence is reinforced relative to other questions' confidence, not against its own binary correctness label. On math, CARE increases the coverage at 5% risk (C@5) across MATH-500, GSM8K, and Big-Math-Digits from 0.8% to 42.4%, compared to existing pointwise correctness-matching rewards. When trained on multi-hop Wikipedia QA from HotpotQA, CARE improves C@5 in out-of-distribution general-knowledge, reasoning, and math benchmarks (including CommonsenseQA, GPQA, SimpleQA, and TriviaQA) from 12.3% to 23.6%.

  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25296
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25296

  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Diffusion Transformers generate high-quality images, but iterative sampling remains computationally expensive. In this paper, we propose CalibCache, a lightweight offline calibration method that corrects systematic discrepancies between cached and full-compute feature dynamics. Specifically, CalibCache calibrates feature acceleration, defined as the second-order finite difference across sampling steps. These maps are then applied during sampling without additional full-network evaluations or online optimization. Across FLUX.1-dev and Qwen-Image on DrawBench and DiT-XL/2 on ImageNet-256, CalibCache consistently improves fidelity or generation quality over TaylorSeer under 20-step sampling at cache intervals $N=3,4,5$.   • Abstract: Split federated learning (SFL) distributes neural networks across clients and a server, coupling class-specific knowledge across their representations. We propose SplitReAlign, a partition-aware representation realignment framework for class-wise SFU. Explanations are simplified approximations of a language model's internal workings. The goal is to obtain explanations that reflect the underlying computation as closely as possible (faithfulness), are understandable to users (plausibility), and help them achieve their intended goals (utility). Second, it proposes two techniques that generate natural language explanations designed to be faithful to the model's prediction process while remaining concise, coherent, and helpful for understanding model decisions. Finally, a study of human-AI interaction explores the roles of evidence and explanations in helping users validate model verdicts in a fact-checking setting.

Title: Verified Netlist-to-RTL Recovery with LLM Agents

  • Authors: Ahmet Efe, Recep Bugra Uludag, Ulya R. Karpuzcu
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25324
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25324
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25324]] Verified Netlist-to-RTL Recovery with LLM Agents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25324)
  • Keywords: attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Gate-level netlists circulate among IP integrators and foundries and can be extracted from chips. Reverse engineering them into readable register-transfer-level (RTL) code would help an adversary understand and modify a design, but remains difficult because word-level operations are scattered across many gates. Large language models (LLMs) can write and optimize RTL, but often fail to recover it from a whole netlist. We propose LOCA (LOgic Cone Attack), an LLM-based attack that divides an anonymized netlist into logic cones, has parallel agents rewrite them, and assembles compact RTL. Each cone contains the complete next-state logic of a group of related registers, giving an agent a small, meaningful task. Of five partitioning methods tested, only grouped cones lead to recovery. LOCA keeps only RTL proven equivalent to the netlist and measures its quality by compression. Function-preserving obfuscation does not stop LOCA on the tested designs, whereas unknown keys or hidden cell functions prevent it from proving recovery of the original design. Across 21 model-design pairs, LOCA recovers 17, versus 6 with whole-netlist prompting. Per-run recovery across models rises from 5% to 74%, at a median cost below one dollar with a low-cost model.

Title: Now You See Me Now You Don't: A Framework To Detect and Reduce Proxy Capacity

  • Authors: Nirek Sharma
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25327
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25327
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25327]] Now You See Me Now You Don't: A Framework To Detect and Reduce Proxy Capacity(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25327)
  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: In regulated domains such as lending, the two most salient fairness concepts are disparate impact and disparate treatment: disparate impact is generally defined by differential rates of adverse outcomes between demographic groups, while disparate treatment is generally defined by the use of demographic information or proxies to issue decisions. There already exist established methods to quantitatively detect and mitigate disparate impact; however, there has been less research on doing the same for disparate treatment. From a practitioner's perspective, the manner in which a conditional independence test is formulated and operationalized to identify proxies is nuanced and underexplored. As Machine Learning (ML) systems increase in scope and application in regulated domains, practitioners need a defensible framework to identify proxies at scale. Current disparate treatment frameworks typically rely upon some combination of quantitative and qualitative review of models' inputs to identify proxies. Features that are identified as proxies are then typically removed from the model in practice, and predictive performance degrades in favor of fairness through unawareness. Here, we propose a two-part framework that reduces the disparate treatment risk of a feature and keeps the predictive performance that removal of the feature loses. First, we propose a quantitative test for proxy capacity that identifies whether some of a feature's values reveal membership in a particular demographic group. Second, we propose a pre-processing methodology that adjusts those proxy features to reduce their proxy capacity. We show empirically on synthetic and public datasets that this framework identifies features that proxy for demographic information and reduces their proxy capacity while the adjusted feature retains task-relevant signal (i.e. improves predictive performance versus removal).

  • Abstract: Generating realistic 3D assets remains constrained by classical representations: surface meshes and compact physically based materials struggle to express objects whose appearance arises from fine geometry or complex light transport, while existing Gaussian-splatting generators typically bake fixed illumination into appearance. A feed-forward large reconstruction model initialized from a pretrained video diffusion backbone then maps the video-VAE latent frames of selected turntable frames directly to millions of anisotropic Gaussians with learned neural appearance features. To preserve high-frequency appearance while learning geometry, we introduce depth-anchored appearance training, and we make million-Gaussian reconstruction practical through foreground token selection and cost-aware distributed sampling. VideoRNG generates complex assets such as fur, feathers, and spider webs that can be interactively rendered, relit, and integrated into scenes.

  • Subjects: cs.CV

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25371
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25371]] A Unified Framework for Image Manipulation Localization Under Multi-Type Weak Supervision(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25371)
  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: Existing weakly supervised image manipulation localization (IML) methods typically rely on annotation-specific training strategies, hindering the joint use of diverse annotations within a single model. This limits the scale of usable training data and constrains further progress. We propose UniIML, a teacher-student framework that maps diverse weak annotations into a unified representation to guide the Segment Anything Model (SAM) in generating multiple candidate masks. However, SAM is designed for general-purpose segmentation and has limited sensitivity to manipulation traces; its predicted quality scores alone may therefore fail to reliably identify the candidate that best delineates the manipulated region. We introduce Ordinal Safe Consensus (OSC), which first filters candidates using trusted weak-annotation constraints encoded in the unified representation. It then ranks the remaining candidates separately by consistency with weak annotations, SAM-predicted quality scores, and cross-view stability, aggregating these rankings for an initial selection. To assess how changes in the evaluation criteria affect candidate selection, OSC repeats ranking and selection while omitting one criterion at a time. The most frequently selected candidate is adopted as the final mask, and its relative selection frequency quantifies selection stability. We construct soft pseudo-labels from the selected candidate's predicted probabilities and estimate pixel-level supervision weights based on selection stability, pixel-level agreement among candidates, and predictive certainty. The student receives direct supervision in regions defined by weak annotations and learns from soft pseudo-labels elsewhere through reliability-weighted distillation, enabling unified training across weak annotation types. Experiments show that UniIML achieves superior average performance compared with state-of-the-art IML methods.

Title: Text Lives in the Guidance: Preventing Silent Text Rewriting in Cached Diffusion Transformers

  • Authors: Kun Zhou, Wenmian Yang, Zhensheng Wang, Weijia Jia
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25382
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25382
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25382]] Text Lives in the Guidance: Preventing Silent Text Rewriting in Cached Diffusion Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25382)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Diffusion transformers (DiTs) now render long, legible text in images, and feature caching is a standard way to accelerate them. Caches are judged by image-level metrics, which do not check whether the rendered text is still correct. We find that caching silently rewrites this text. On Z-Image, TaylorSeer at 2.9x fewer FLOPs lowers word accuracy from 62% to 46%, while ImageReward and CLIP Score barely change. Sampling with fewer steps at the same compute keeps almost all of this text, so the loss comes from how the cache predicts the skipped steps. Decomposing the cache error along classifier-free guidance (CFG), we trace this loss mainly to the guidance term, the difference between the conditional and unconditional predictions, which caches extrapolate worst on text. Correcting the guidance inside the text alone recovers most of the lost text. We therefore propose GuideCache, which recomputes both CFG branches only on the tokens with the largest predicted guidance error, using the guidance terms that the cache already stores. At 2.4x fewer FLOPs, GuideCache restores full-compute prompt fidelity on Z-Image and Qwen-Image, and on Z-Image it renders text as well as or better than ToCa, TeaCache, and FasterCache while staying much closer to the full model's output. GuideCache works with caches of any order, and on top of fewer sampling steps it keeps more text than step reduction at a higher speedup. These results suggest that acceleration methods for text-rendering models should be evaluated on the text they render.

Title: Drifting, Discriminating, and Directioning for Action Coordination in Higher-dimensional Action Space

  • Authors: Taisuke Kobayashi
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25385
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25385
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25385]] Drifting, Discriminating, and Directioning for Action Coordination in Higher-dimensional Action Space(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25385)
  • Keywords: extraction
  • Abstract: This paper proposes a lightweight policy model to mitigate the difficulty of handling higher-dimensional action spaces in reinforcement learning (RL), alongside an algorithm to effectively train it. To address this, we focus on drifting models, which can implicitly represent complex distributions and infer actions in a single forward pass. However, while drifting models can be straightforwardly trained via backpropagation of the Q-gradient, this alone cannot be expected to appropriately capture the desired action coordination. Therefore, this paper constructs a three-step learning algorithm to encourage the action coordination. First, the source distribution of the drifting model is co-optimized as a diagonal policy model. This conditions the drifting model to attach the coordination, which cannot be expressed by a diagonal distribution, as residuals. Second, a discriminator model before and after drifting is introduced with adversarial training for a regularization term. This actively promotes the extraction of the coordination that fundamentally separates the two distributions. Third, best-of-N sampling is applied for action selection during rollouts in order to give directionality toward/beyond the regions where the coordination is insufficiently extracted/sufficiently discovered. The proposed method consistently improves the sample efficiency and the final control performance over the conventional diagonal distribution modeling across three locomotion tasks with high-dimensional action spaces. Furthermore, the analysis indicates that this improvement is achieved by reducing variance in certain action dimensions, effectively coupling them with other actions as action coordination.

Title: TuringSpace: Grounding Spatial Intelligence in Action with Interactive CAPTCHAs

  • Authors: Xuwei Ding, Skylar Zhai, Linxin Song, Jiarui Feng, Dongqi Fu, Huajun Xi, Junnan Li, Jingcheng Liang, Jieyu Zhao, Yinglong Xia, Hong Li, Jian Kang

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25387
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25387
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25387]] TuringSpace: Grounding Spatial Intelligence in Action with Interactive CAPTCHAs(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25387)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Humans act in the visual world by inferring the 3D layout behind a 2D view, predicting how an action changes it, and correcting themselves after seeing the result. Can multimodal large language models (MLLMs) do the same? CAPTCHAs offer a natural test, since humans solve them through this very loop, clicking and dragging on the screen and adjusting after each result. Yet existing CAPTCHA benchmarks are largely solved and rarely spatial, while spatial benchmarks rarely require action. We therefore present TuringSpace, a synthetic web environment of 125 interactive CAPTCHAs that span five spatial abilities and must be solved through clicks and drags on the screen. Every task is a program that generates new instances with a checker and an oracle, so the tasks also form a scalable training gym. Humans solve 94.1% of the tasks, whereas the best model, GPT-6 Astra, still fails 25.9% of them, and the best open-weight model, Kimi K3, fails 68.0%. Our further analysis shows that most failures come from misreading the screenshot, and that model reasoning rarely corrects a wrong belief but hallucinates evidence to defend it. To test whether such spatial abilities can be learned, we fine-tune Qwen3.5-9B on this gym. Its success rate rises from 1.6% to 33.1%, above Kimi K3, and it improves on all seven out-of-distribution benchmarks, including a 9.9-point gain on the real-world indoor videos of VSI-Bench. These results show that grounding spatial intelligence in screen actions exposes what MLLMs lack and offers a scalable way to teach it, with gains that transfer to real-world scenes. We will release TuringSpace to support further study.

  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: High-impact attacks on PHP web applications often chain limited-capability vulnerabilities with other flaws or intended behaviors. Discovering these chains requires identifying flaws and the dependencies enabling capability composition. Existing vulnerability discovery techniques analyze flaws in isolation, while attack planning assumes known components. This separation makes it difficult to discover previously unknown components whose security relevance becomes apparent only through their potential roles in an exploit chain. We present ChainAgent, a goal-directed LLM-agent framework that jointly discovers exploit-chain components and their capability dependencies in source code. Guided by an empirical study of 386 real-world web exploit chains, ChainAgent represents vulnerabilities and functional gadgets--intended program behaviors that can be repurposed in attacks--through preconditions and capabilities over security-relevant objects. Instead of discovering vulnerabilities first and constructing attack paths afterward, ChainAgent uses an evolving exploit graph to guide source-code inspection. It searches backward from unmet preconditions for capability producers and forward from discovered capabilities for functional gadgets that consume or amplify them. Object-centered chaining knowledge from our empirical study directs searches toward promising vulnerability classes, functional operations, and object accesses. We evaluate ChainAgent against Codex, a general-purpose coding agent, on 15 PHP applications across three shared LLM backends. ChainAgent achieves 42.02%--44.63% recall for goal-reaching components and recovers up to 4.4 times as many correct goal-reaching dependency edges as Codex. An additional evaluation of 30 real-world PHP applications identifies 20 vulnerabilities and 13 exploit chains, nine of which achieve remote code execution (RCE).
