Title: Interference Bounds and Merge-Aware Training Dynamics for Composable Fine-Tuned Models

  • Authors: Alireza Moayedikia
  • Subjects: cs.LG, math.OC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25430
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25430
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25430]] Interference Bounds and Merge-Aware Training Dynamics for Composable Fine-Tuned Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25430)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Combining fine-tuned models by composing their parameter updates has become a standard alternative to multi-task training. We formalize composability as a property of a collection of task vectors and prove that the excess loss of the merged model is controlled by an interference functional of the cross-task curvature overlap and the signed alignment of the updates. In the quadratic regime this functional equals the excess loss, giving a criterion that is necessary as well as sufficient and that measures the angle between task vectors in the curvature metric rather than the Euclidean one. Fine-tuning under a curvature-guided regularizer -- constructed from curvature statistics of the kind memory-efficient optimizers maintain -- keeps displacement in the base curvature metric below a ceiling set by the penalty coefficient at every iterate, at the standard $O(1/\sqrt{T})$ non-convex rate. Under a single Loewner comparison between the base statistic and the task curvatures, this control converts into a ceiling on the interference functional, inversely proportional to the penalty coefficient, so that the curvature term of the bound is controlled during training. A first-order term, present because regularized solutions are not stationary for the task loss, is not reduced in the same way. Experiments on an exact non-quadratic model confirm the budget and the ceiling, show the first-order term dominating for strong penalties, and find the curvature-guided penalty slightly better than an isotropic one. In a three-seed transformer study the functional is rank-correlated with merging degradation, but no more strongly than the Euclidean displacement norm.

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25452
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25452
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25452]] FamilySpace: From Emergent Chemical Semantics to Controllable Crystal Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25452)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Latent crystal generative models typically combine a variational autoencoder (VAE) for learning atom-wise representations with a flow model that transports a simple source distribution to the learned latent distribution. However, previous studies have mainly focused on the quality of generated crystals, leaving the structure and generative utility of the atom-wise latent space largely unexplored. In this work, we show that even a vanilla crystal VAE implicitly organizes atom-wise latent representations according to elemental families, with different families occupying largely separated regions of the latent space. We term this phenomenon emergent chemical semantics. This structure suggests that sampling from a family-specific latent region can bias the decoder toward atoms from the corresponding elemental family. Building on this insight, we propose FamilySpace, a framework for controlling the elemental-family composition of generated crystals. FamilySpace assigns each elemental family a distinct source component, forming a family-indexed multi-source distribution, and trains a flow model using source-target pairs matched by family. In inference, a user-specified family template selects the source component for each atom-wise representation, enabling direct control over the family composition of generated crystals. Experiments on MP-20 and MPTS-52 show that FamilySpace achieves competitive crystal-generation performance while matching prescribed elemental-family compositions. Moreover, selected family templates increase the fraction of generated crystals predicted to be stable or metastable, demonstrating that latent-space composition control can support targeted exploration of promising chemical regions without additional optimization techniques such as reinforcement learning. Title: Beyond Resemblance: Image Fusion Reimagined

  • Authors: Hao Zhang, Yumin Huang, Linfeng Tang, Junjun Jiang, Jiayi Ma

  • Keywords: generative, segmentation
  • Abstract: Image fusion often lacks ground-truth fused images, forcing existing deep methods to indirectly define fusion objectives by requiring the fused image to simultaneously resemble multiple source images. However, the distinct imaging characteristics of different modalities can impose competing constraints on the fused result, potentially limiting the effective integration of their complementary information. To address this long-standing challenge facing the image fusion community, we propose ApexFuse, a novel generative image fusion paradigm that moves beyond source-resemblance constraints toward learning a general distribution of high-quality scene representations. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ApexFuse harmoniously integrates salient thermal targets, natural colors, and fine textures, achieving superior visual quality as well as semantic segmentation and object detection performance over previous source-resemblance methods. Title: Lossless Latent Diffusion Models

  • Authors: Tongda Xu, Yu Zhang, Jun Hu, Huijuan Huang
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25454
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25454
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25454]] Lossless Latent Diffusion Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25454)
  • Keywords: diffusion, generative
  • Abstract: We study the problem of training image diffusion models while preserving pixel-exact reconstruction. Prior works typically address this problem by directly modeling images in pixel space, requiring modifications to the diffusion training objective or model architecture. In this work, we introduce a different paradigm: rather than modifying diffusion models, we construct a lossless transformation that maps pixels into a representation more amenable to diffusion modeling. We term our approach the Lossless Latent Diffusion Model (LLDM). We construct LLDM using residual latent transformation and uneven latent scaling, and explain its effectiveness through a proposed metric, semantic rank priority (SRP). Empirically, LLDM substantially outperforms prior pixel-space diffusion models in generation quality and even surpasses several widely used lossy VAEs. Furthermore, we show that SRP is strongly correlated with downstream generative performance.

  • Abstract: Recent work reports strong pragmatic performance by large language models (LLMs) in English, yet whether this performance generalises across languages and evaluation settings remains unclear. We also explore cross-view consistency checks as a mitigation. These findings expose a gap in visual trust: securing what a machine reads does not secure what a user sees.   • Authors: Zihao Dan, Jinyu Zhang, Jinzhao Lian, Yun Chen, Shinan Liu

  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25470
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25470

  • Keywords: attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as agents that translate user requests into sequences of local tool invocations. While prior cache-based side-channel attacks target the locally deployed models, they typically require the adversary to share model-related components or hardware resources with the victim's inference process. These assumptions do not apply to the predominant agentic settings, in which commercial LLMs execute on vendor-controlled infrastructure. We show that agentic execution exposes a more practical attack surface: although inference remains remote, tools are executed on the user's machine and commonly rely on shared system libraries. In this paper, we first systematically evaluate Flush+Reload and Prime+Probe for monitoring agentic tool invocations. We evaluate the Tool Recovery Attack on 11 LLMs across Codex and Claude Code. It consistently reconstructs tool workflows, with the lowest F1 score exceeding 93%. We further demonstrate two attack scenarios enabled by the recovered traces. Task Profiling Attack infers user intent across two Terminal-Bench task categories and the Trigger Localization Attack identifies sensitive tool calls in encryption/decryption and model training/inference workloads. Even under substantial system noise on a server shared by approximately 20 users, both Task Profiling Attack and Trigger Localization Attack maintain high accuracy.

  • Authors: Bo Li, Taoyong Cui, Bob Zhang, Mengran Li, Chengyang Zhang, Yang Zhang, Pheng Ann Heng, Wanli Ouyang, Zhenfei Yin, Yingcheng Wu, Ling Yang

  • Abstract: Neural networks encode learned capabilities in their weights, yet extending those capabilities with explicit task knowledge remains a fundamental challenge. WeightCode, a WSP framework, uses a frozen language model to write executable weight programs that install new computations into trained neural networks using task knowledge and their existing weights, without gradient-based training. Six programs written for an already edited network add 5.23-14.50 points of contrast robustness while retaining the previously installed noise gain. Together, these results establish WSP as a new route to acquiring and accumulating capabilities in trained neural networks through reusable weight programs.

Title: Persona Lenses: What Makes a Vision-Language Model See Through Its Persona's Eyes?

  • Authors: Yoonho Shin
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.HC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25508
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25508
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25508]] Persona Lenses: What Makes a Vision-Language Model See Through Its Persona's Eyes?(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25508)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Vision-language models have been used to simulate where a user looks on a web page. However, a persona in the prompt reaches the simulated gaze only partly. We measure this reach as the difference in one model's gaze between two MatrAIx personas that differ in a single attribute. We introduce persona lenses, which place the persona between the model and the page: a text lens (a two-step prompt in which the model first writes what the persona would look at), a pixel lens (marks on the region matching the persona's interests) and a weight lens (a dwell reward gated to keep the scanpath natural). Using Qwen3-VL (4B and 32B) and Qwen3.5-9B on generated and live pages, we compare the lenses with one another and with scale, the 32B model with no lens. It has been confirmed that the text lens separates the personas strongly but is not robust to profile changes that should not move gaze. The pixel lens separates the personas less but is unaffected by attributes unrelated to gaze. At 4B and 9B, the weight lens separates the personas on interests held out from training, with no extra step on a new page. The 32B model separates the personas with the base prompt alone. Therefore, the choice of a lens depends on cost and model size.

  • Subjects: cs.CV

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Video Large Language Models process large numbers of visual tokens, making token pruning important for efficient inference. Training-free pruning methods differ in whether and how they use the user query, and the value of query conditioning remains unclear. We present a controlled study of training-free, pre-LLM video token selection. Using a shared Qwen-3-VL backbone, token budgets, and decoding settings, we compare 21 pruning principles spanning query-agnostic, shallow query-conditioned, structured query-conditioned, and holistic strategies. To instantiate the last two categories, we introduce Structured Query Pruning, which decomposes the question into semantic anchors, and a Pareto variant, which combines anchor relevance with query-agnostic visual diversity. However, query access is not inherently advantageous: strong query-agnostic coverage methods remain competitive or superior in the full-field comparison. Relative to a redundancy-only baseline, structured selection helps most on evidence-specific and long-context tasks at tight budgets; on broad tasks, the holistic selector improves over either relevance or redundancy alone. Further, pruning reduces cost and memory. These results support a practical design rule: preserve visual coverage by default, and add structured relevance when the task and token budget demand selective evidence retrieval.

Title: Towards Autoregressive Neural Network Parameter Generation

  • Authors: Bedionita Soro, Bruno Andreis, Song Chong, Sung Ju Hwang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25538

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently established a new paradigm in machine learning, demonstrating exceptional zero-shot generalization and complex reasoning capabilities. Concurrently, neural network parameter generation has emerged as a promising avenue for rapid task adaptation and model synthesis. Existing weight generation techniques, however, struggle to jointly learn the distribution of diverse models, to encode heterogeneous architectures within a single generator, and to generalize out of distribution. We therefore ask whether pretrained parameters can be encoded into a discrete domain so that the zero-shot generalization LLMs exhibit in text generation can be transferred to weight generation. Following this view, we propose an LLM-inspired approach, Instruction-Guided Parameter Generation (IGPG), that formulates network parameter generation as an autoregressive modeling problem. We first quantize pretrained weights with a VQ-VAE, then train a prefix-conditioned autoregressive generator over the resulting tokens. At inference, the generated weight tokens are mapped back to parameter space by the VQ-VAE decoder. We evaluate the framework across in-distribution generation, cross-dataset transfer, and cross-architecture settings. The proposed method achieves competitive performance in in-distribution generation and provides useful parameter initializations for held-out datasets and architectural configurations, improving downstream performance over random initialization after adaptation. These results suggest that discrete autoregressive modeling provides a promising framework for learning and transferring structure across collections of pretrained neural network parameters.

Title: Anchored Inertial Proposals with Objective Verification for Diffusion-Prior Inverse Problems

  • Authors: Minwoo Kim, Seunghyeok Shin, Dabin Kim, Hongki Lim
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25551
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25551
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25551]] Anchored Inertial Proposals with Objective Verification for Diffusion-Prior Inverse Problems(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25551)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Training-free diffusion priors are powerful for inverse problems, but measurement guidance during reverse sampling is local: aggressive updates can improve immediate data fit while disrupting later denoising. We introduce an anchored inertial correction rule that separates proposal generation from acceptance. At each corrected step, the denoiser prediction anchors a frozen clean-space objective combining measurement fit with a noise-level-dependent proximal term. Heavy-ball dynamics with step size and momentum computed from the measured curvature generate a clean-state candidate; a separate verifier evaluates it with the same objective, with the denoiser prediction retained as fallback; both stages are derived from one objective for the whole trajectory, considered one step at a time. The accepted clean state is re-noised to continue sampling. The rule applies to pixel and latent diffusion with differentiable measurement operators, requires no retraining, and adds no denoiser calls inside the correction loop. We analyze how objective verification yields local nonincrease and displacement control. Across natural-image benchmarks, the method achieves competitive reconstruction quality. Code is available at this https URL.

Title: UniReFP: Robust Unified Fingerprinting for Vision Models against Cross-Task Repurposing Attacks

  • Authors: Ziye Geng, Guang Yang, Yihang Chen, Lingfeng Yao, Xing Shi, Miao Pan, Xin Fu, Changqing Luo
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25555
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25555
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25555]] UniReFP: Robust Unified Fingerprinting for Vision Models against Cross-Task Repurposing Attacks(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25555)
  • Keywords: protect, attack, robust
  • Abstract: We identify, for the first time, a new model modification attack--the cross-task model repurposing attack--that can render fingerprints generated by existing model fingerprinting approaches inapplicable. To address this challenge, we propose UniReFP--a novel unified model fingerprinting framework applicable to diverse vision models across different tasks--that utilizes ownership evidence in a shared class-level semantic space rather than original task-specific output space, enabling effective ownership verification when a protected model is repurposed across tasks with heterogeneous output formats. Specifically, UniReFP constructs a surrogate classifier by repurposing the protected model, enabling fingerprints to be generated independently of the original task-dependent output space of the protected model. Moreover, it optimizes grouped fingerprints to encode ownership evidence: fingerprints within the same group are encouraged to induce consistent semantic responses and intermediate representations on the surrogate classifier, while producing dispersed responses on independently trained reference classifiers. During verification, UniReFP uses a task-aware semantic projection mechanism to map heterogeneous suspect-model outputs into unified semantic-presence vectors, and determines ownership by measuring inner-group consistency. Extensive experimental results validate the effectiveness of UniReFP, consistently showing superior performance under cross-task repurposing attacks, commonly studied model modification attacks, and even their combinations.

Title: Is a Description Worth a Thousand Demonstrations? Specificity and Reliability of Task Descriptions in In-Context Learning

  • Authors: Bruce Nguyen, Bei Jiang, Linglong Kong
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25560
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25560
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25560]] Is a Description Worth a Thousand Demonstrations? Specificity and Reliability of Task Descriptions in In-Context Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25560)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: A prompt for in-context learning (ICL) can describe a task in addition to, or instead of, demonstrating it through examples. Yet how task descriptions work remains poorly understood theoretically. Through the Bayesian view of ICL, we model descriptions as informative priors characterized by their specificity and reliability. We adapt the effective sample size (ESS) from Bayesian statistics, quantifying a description's predictive value as an equivalent number of demonstrations. For in-context linear regression, we show that a reliable description's ESS grows with its specificity and with the task's dimension. A description that may be irrelevant, however, gains little from its specificity unless demonstrations are given. In practice, do in-context learners use descriptions as a Bayes-optimal predictor would? Our experiments show that meta-trained Transformers come close to the Bayes-optimal ESS when tested on their training distribution. Pretrained language models, however, make too little use of highly specific descriptions when answering without reasoning; most of them closely match the Bayes-optimal predictor only when reasoning is enabled. These results extend the Bayesian analysis of ICL from demonstrations to descriptions.

Title: Passing NPCR Without Diffusion: Exact Signatures, a Detection Benchmark and an Audit of Published Image Ciphers

  • Authors: Mua'ad Alfarajat
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.MM, eess.IV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25561
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25561
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25561]] Passing NPCR Without Diffusion: Exact Signatures, a Detection Benchmark and an Audit of Published Image Ciphers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25561)
  • Keywords: security, diffusion
  • Abstract: NPCR, the share of ciphertext bytes changed by a one-pixel plaintext change, is reported for nearly every image cipher as evidence of diffusion, with 99.61% the target. Several mechanisms produce values near it, and the mean does not say which. We separate three regimes: no propagation, one round of forward and backward chaining linear over addition or XOR, and independent ciphertexts. For the second an exact count applies. After a least-significant-bit change its mean asymptotically matches the independent-ciphertext reference, while its spread without cancellation is about five to twenty times larger. In simulation, the one-sided NPCR test of Wu, Noonan and Agaian flags one-round additive, mixed and S-box chaining in 2-4% of papers; read in both directions, the same five values flag at least 99.9%. Two-sided testing does the detecting; the spread and the other signatures help diagnose the structure. We then audit twenty-one published schemes. The count matches a reimplementation of one published diffusion layer on every trial and accounts for its reported table, the signatures show two schemes whose printed specifications cannot produce their reported values, and find that three of our own report plain-versus-cipher values and fall to two chosen images; ten more change their encryption material with the plaintext or per encryption, so their NPCR mostly reflects that change. A released checker applies the signatures to published tables, showing what a table measures within stated assumptions; it does not certify security.

  • Abstract: An assistant monitoring a person performing a procedure is most useful when it can detect mistakes while there is still time to recover. In this paper, we study online, instruction-conditioned mistake detection in egocentric videos.   • Keywords: fair, large language model
  • Abstract: Recent benchmark evaluations increasingly rely on Large Language Models (LLMs) as automatic judges. This paradigm assumes LLMs evaluate based on the rubric's semantic meaning rather than its numeric scale. We question this assumption by introducing semantic faithfulness: the principle that semantically equivalent rubrics must yield consistent scores. To quantify this, we propose a scale-reversal probe that inverts score anchors without altering evaluation criteria. Furthermore, we introduce floor normalization to calibrate reversal disagreement against intrinsic model variability, enabling fair cross-model comparisons. Finally, we demonstrate that bidirectional anchor averaging reduces dependence on any single anchor direction without compromising alignment with human judgments. Our findings emphasize that trustworthy LLM judges must reliably follow rubric semantics, not just correlate with human ratings.

Title: Bradley-Terry Training Can Distort Outcome Probabilities of Initially Accurate Language World Models

  • Authors: Xinze Shi, Litian Zhang, Zhenjie Su, Zelong Yu, Yuting Zhang, Qihua Feng, Zifeng Kang, Chaozhuo Li, Zhengxuan Hu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25594
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25594
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25594]] Bradley-Terry Training Can Distort Outcome Probabilities of Initially Accurate Language World Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25594)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Comparative feedback, in which a judge says which of two candidate outcomes is closer to what happened, is a practical way to train language world models that assign probabilities to an action's outcomes. We show that, fitted with a Bradley-Terry objective, it need not teach the true outcome probabilities and can distort accurate ones. On a simulated robot push with enumerated outcome distributions, this objective takes a model that is accurate on unseen layouts (hazard error 2.4 percentage points, pp) and moves 33 to 43 pp of its probability onto outcomes that none of a condition's enumerated branches produces. The cause is the objective, not the comparisons: its optimum, computed before training, predicts where the model ends up, and a likelihood that knows the judge's rule learns from the same comparisons without the shift. The shift is robust: it recurs on unseen conditions, under five candidate laws and a three-fold denser enumeration, and about 29 pp remain without the physically impossible events. Its cost is conditional: with those events it inflates hazard probabilities, and a threshold inherited from the pre-feedback model costs about 3 pp of utility per decision on trained and 1 to 2 pp on unseen conditions, through false rejects; without them the utility loss averages below 1 pp over six seeds, though a small regret in action choice remains. Audits of readout, response and decision trace the effect from probabilities to actions.

  • Authors: Haotian Zhai, Leqi Liu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25597

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown promise as predictors across various tasks, benefiting from their ability to use information from unstructured context. However, when predictions serve as parameters of downstream optimization problems, higher prediction accuracy does not necessarily translate into lower decision costs. We propose decision-focused reinforcement learning (DFRL), a framework for post-training LLMs to predict optimization parameters using negative downstream decision regret as reward. DFRL trains LLM predictors to minimize the costs of the decisions induced by their predictions, without requiring downstream convexity or differentiability, and integrates readily into existing RL post-training frameworks. To address expensive reward evaluation, we develop two approaches. Heuristic warm-starting uses inexpensive approximate rewards during an initial phase before switching to refinement with exact rewards. Reward correction combines inexpensive estimates with randomized exact evaluations, preserving the expected policy gradient while reducing the expected number of exact solves per update. Experiments on three synthetic optimization problems demonstrate improvements over prediction-loss baselines, while a larger knapsack experiment shows faster early improvement under short training budgets. On inventory planning with H&M transactions and product descriptions, our best configuration, in which the LLM reasons before predicting, reduces regret by 31.4% relative to the SFT baseline and 12.4% relative to the strongest evaluated conventional forecasting baseline.

Existing defense mechanisms rely on either LLM-based monitors, which lack provable safety guarantees, or stateless policy enforcement layers, which miss multi-step data flows and execution side-effects unmodeled by the policy language (e.g., network calls initiated inside a Python script). We introduce Tether, a framework for runtime enforcement of stateful security policies over the observed side-effects of an agent's actions. Because actions can propagate labels from sources to destinations, a single rule can enforce fine-grained IFC policies across multi-step execution flows of arbitrary length. Expressing policies in Lean enables formal verification that they satisfy global security invariants (e.g., preventing network access after reading a sensitive file). We evaluate Tether on realistic coding-agent benchmarks across both benign and adversarial trajectories. With a single policy shared across all tasks, Tether blocks 100% of in-scope attacks while maintaining a benign false-positive rate under 5%.

Title: PSTO: A PDE-structured transport operator with shared implicit updates for multiscale CDR systems

  • Authors: Kaiwen Li, Dezhi Zhou, Kun Wu, Xingcai Lu
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25607
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25607

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Multiscale convection-diffusion-reaction (CDR) systems couple spatial transport with local stiff reaction kinetics, making their numerical simulation computationally demanding. Conventional neural surrogates require costly coupled CDR training data, and their parameter counts scale with the number of transported variables, limiting scalability to high-dimensional CDR systems. Here we propose a PDE-Structured Transport Operator (PSTO) that learns convection--diffusion dynamics solely from reaction-free data. By embedding the PDE structure into the neural architecture, PSTO generalizes to full CDR systems at inference without retraining. A single transport operator block is shared across all transported variables and refined through relaxed fixed-point iterations, performing an implicit-like correction in latent space. This improves stability during recursive multi-step prediction without adding trainable parameters. We evaluate PSTO on three benchmarks: a canonical 2D CDR system, a multiscale Robertson counterflow flame, and a multiscale hydrogen planar jet flame. In every case, PSTO reduces the mean relative $L_2$ error after 100 coupled steps by at least 50% compared to the strongest neural baseline. Ablation studies on the fixed-point iterations and detailed error decomposition further confirm that PSTO achieves both high one-step accuracy and long-term stability in coupled rollouts. Time-step sensitivity analysis shows that PSTO maintains accuracy even with a tenfold larger time step, whereas baseline accuracies decay rapidly as the time step increases. This framework enables efficient, modular modeling of multiscale CDR dynamics by separating transport learning from the treatment of local kinetics.

Title: DAWN: Robust Neural Blind Deconvolution via Alternating Wiener Guidance

  • Authors: Yida Pan, Yixuan Jia, Chaoyan Huang, Qing Qu, Ismail Alkhouri
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25621
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25621
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25621]] DAWN: Robust Neural Blind Deconvolution via Alternating Wiener Guidance(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25621)
  • Keywords: robust, diffusion, data-free
  • Abstract: Blind image deconvolution (BID) is an ill-posed inverse problem that aims to jointly recover a sharp image and blur kernel from a single degraded observation. Under nontrivial and high noise, which is common in photography and scientific imaging such as microscopy, BID remains particularly challenging, as existing methods suffer from noise overfitting and inaccurate kernel estimation. Existing training-data-free learning methods for BID, such as those based on the deep image prior (DIP), often utilize external guidance by including model-based estimates as additional loss terms, which underexploits the information these estimates carry especially under nontrivial noise. To address these challenges, we propose DAWN, a training-data-free DIP framework that alternates between injecting model-based estimates directly into the model input and refining this imperfect estimate with the network's own prior. To provide more reliable kernel estimates for these injections, we apply autoencoding regularization to both the image and kernel networks. On natural and scientific images under varying levels of Gaussian and Poisson noise, DAWN outperforms state-of-the-art methods in both image and kernel quality (achieving up to +4.5 dB over the next best baseline on the Levin dataset at $\sigma = 0.1$). Without any training images, DAWN remains highly competitive with a state-of-the-art diffusion model on FFHQ, despite the latter being trained on a massive number of in-distribution images.

  • Abstract: Machine translation with large language models requires both high translation quality and low generation latency. Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) can generate tokens at arbitrary target positions through iterative denoising, offering a new paradigm for parallel translation beyond sequential autoregressive decoding. However, existing methods still incur redundant computation: fixed target generation lengths waste computation on unused positions, while confidence-only commitment delays medium-confidence tokens whose predictions have largely stabilized. Title: Extending Rademacher Complexity-Based Generalization Theory to Domain Generalization

  • Authors: Xi Wang, Liang Bai, Xian Yang, Richard Yi Da Xu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25650
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25650
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25650]] Extending Rademacher Complexity-Based Generalization Theory to Domain Generalization(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25650)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: Domain generalization (DG) aims to train models on multiple source domains with distinct distributions to ensure robust performance on unseen target domains. In doing so, it elevates the concept of generalization from the traditional sample level to the distribution level. Existing theoretical frameworks introduce a domain space endowed with a meta-distribution to extend classical generalization analysis to this new setting. However, these approaches typically treat the domain space as devoid of geometry. In reality, the domain space is an inherently infinite-dimensional, nonlinear abstract space composed of probability distributions, and its complex geometric properties fundamentally determine generalization behavior. Consequently, existing geometry-free theories of domain generalization have a fundamental limitation. They lack a formal characterization of what distinguishes distribution-level generalization from sample-level generalization, leaving the inherent difficulty of generalizing across domains largely unexplained. To bridge this gap, we formalize the domain space as the Wasserstein space over the representation space. Building on the geometric properties of this space (specifically the $\mathrm{CD}(K,\infty)$ curvature theory) we derive a novel, geometry-dependent DG error bound. By introducing an additional curvature-dependent confidence term, this bound formally disentangles the two levels of generalization effects and reveals the essential nature that domain generalization is affected by both the invariance and the curvature of the representation space. Finally, we experimentally validate the effect of curvature on domain generalization.

  • Authors: Xin Jin, Maximillian Liao, Handong Chen, Yi Nancy Li, Junyu Liu

  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25663

  • Abstract: A quantum computer running Shor's algorithm can extract the private key of every Ethereum account whose public key is published, and accounts exposed by their own transactions hold 54.6% of execution-layer ETH. Title: Explainability of genotype embedding models through observed cohort paths

  • Authors: Amashi Niwarthana, Aloysius Tan, Jagath C. Rajapakse
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25681
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25681
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25681]] Explainability of genotype embedding models through observed cohort paths(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25681)
  • Keywords: explainability
  • Abstract: Deep learning models that combine genotypes with neuroimaging predict neurological diseases more accurately than either modality alone. Their clinical use, however, requires identifying which genetic variants a prediction is based on. Path-based attribution methods like Integrated Gradients address this by integrating the model's gradient from a baseline to the input. These methods assume the model accepts any point along that path as a valid input. Genotype models violate this assumption. They map the three discrete allele dosages \{0,1,2\} to vectors through a learned embedding defined only at those values, so a straight line passes through fractional genotypes at which no embedding, and therefore no gradient, exists. Attribution must therefore proceed in embedding space where the choice of path can affect the resulting attribution. We show that the commonly used per-variant estimator does not generally satisfy completeness. Additive separability guarantees completeness for this estimator, but interactions between variants generally break that property. Instead, we integrate along piecewise-linear embedding-space paths connecting genotype waypoints observed in the training cohort. Each path satisfies completeness in the continuum although the interiors of its segments are not necessarily observed. Our method improves faithfulness over Integrated Gradients and discretized Integrated Gradients under both perturbation protocols, achieves the strongest results for Parkinson's disease classification under support-preserving perturbations, and is competitive with a support-background Shapley baseline under per-variant perturbations. It also shows higher seed stability and better recovery of planted non-additive effects in a synthetic benchmark.

  • Authors: Yanbo Li, An Xiang, Xiaoxin Hu, Feng Wang, Cheng-zhong Xu, Kejiang Ye, Xitong Gao
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25689

  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25689]] D3-Cache: Accelerating Diffusion Models with Dynamic Mode Decomposition(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25689)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Diffusion Transformers generate high-quality images and videos, but every sampling step requires a full network evaluation. Training-free caching reduces this cost by reusing or forecasting network outputs across steps, but common approaches use per-model calibrated skip criteria or maintain per-layer prediction histories. We present D3-Cache, which applies Dynamic Mode Decomposition (DMD) to Diffusion sampling: it fits DMD to a short history of exact velocities and forecasts only the velocity that the scheduler consumes. A horizon-aware gate decides when to skip: whenever the network is evaluated, its output is compared with the forecast for that step, and the larger the error, the sooner the network is evaluated again, up to a cap on consecutive skipped steps. D3-Cache requires no training or per-model offline calibration curve and adds negligible memory. It shares one history length and risk tolerance across the four evaluated models. On four state-of-the-art image and video models, D3-Cache achieves speedups of 2.6-3.8x with high fidelity to the base model output.

  • Authors: Zhenhao Tian, Jintong Yu, Jingwei Feng, Jianwei Zhuge, Penghui Li
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25718
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25718
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25718]] Hardening JavaScript Programs against LLM-Based Vulnerability Detection(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25718)
  • Keywords: protect, defense, attack
  • Abstract: JavaScript programs are often distributed as source code, so attackers can analyze them for vulnerabilities. LLM-based vulnerability detection agents now make this analysis much easier. To mitigate this emerging threat, we present AntiAgent, a defense that lets developers harden their programs against such detection agents. AntiAgent transforms a program before release to impede an agent's analysis. To find effective transformations, AntiAgent evolves a corpus of programs with known vulnerabilities and traces how a detection agent analyzes each evolved version. It keeps the versions on which the agent misses or misreports the vulnerability and extracts their transformations into reusable rules. To protect a new program, AntiAgent carefully divides the task between an LLM and the rules, so that the transformations adapt to the program while preserving its semantics. We evaluate AntiAgent on real-world JavaScript applications against four detection agents, including Claude Code and Codex. AntiAgent reduces the number of vulnerabilities that each agent detects by 52.0-60.7%, outperforming the conventional obfuscator JS-Obfuscator and the LLM-based transformation method FPA on every agent. The transformed programs pass all project tests, indicating that AntiAgent preserves the original program semantics. The transformation rules also remain effective on vulnerability types absent from the corpus and on programs written in another language like Python.

Title: SteerTrace: Tracing How Target Attacks Steer World Action Models

  • Authors: Yukun Dai, Tianshi Wang, Fengling Li, Jingjing Li, Xin Li, Lei Zhu
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25719
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25719
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25719]] SteerTrace: Tracing How Target Attacks Steer World Action Models(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25719)
  • Keywords: attack
  • Abstract: World action models (WAMs) predict future videos and actions with one backbone, and their imagined future is often seen as a safety signal. We introduce SteerTrace, a targeted universal attack on WAMs that also traces its effect inside the model. One $\ell_\infty$-bounded (4/255) perturbation per model, shared across frames, tasks, and camera views, pushes the executed action toward an attacker-chosen target. On eight WAMs from four families (FastWAM, Faster-WAM, OpenWAM and LingBot-VA) on LIBERO and RoboTwin, it drives task success close to zero and moves the attacked action toward the target. Same-budget noise and BadWAM produce little action shift in the target direction. We use the signed targeted effect to measure how much of the effect each intervention removes or reproduces. Under the standard attack, restoring future tokens removes 61-94% of the targeted effect in the five models whose action experts attend to them. When the attack is constrained to keep the predicted future close to the clean prediction, the targeted effect can become more dependent on current-frame tokens. In three of these five models, restoring current-frame tokens removes 87-99% of the effect, compared with 9-15% for future tokens. In six models, restoring just 0.36% of the FFN neurons in layers 10-29 of the video expert removes 72-96% of the targeted effect. Residual directions 2-16 hold only 11-18% of the shift energy, yet removing them cancels most of the targeted effect.

Title: Closing Performance Gap for Diffusion Language Models under Low Inference Budgets

  • Authors: Hao Wu, Sipeng Chen, Jingwen Fu, Yuwang Wang

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion language models offer an architectural route to low-budget generation by updating multiple tokens in parallel, which can reduce the number of sequential decoding steps relative to token-by-token autoregressive decoding. However, realizing this advantage at low budgets is challenging, as generation quality often declines when the number of denoising and refinement steps is reduced. Editable diffusion models address this quality loss through post-draft refinement, but useful intermediate results may still be lost before final submission. We introduce CaRR (Commitment-aware Refinement and Recovery), a training-free framework which improves submission quality from a fixed editable checkpoint through updated-context refill and trajectory-based recovery within its existing forward schedule. Submission Commitment Recovery (SCR) uses the resulting refinement trajectory to recover supported candidates when the final response does not satisfy task-specific output requirements. Both modules operate within the native mask/edit schedule without additional backbone forwards. Experiments on code generation and mathematical reasoning show consistent gains in average accuracy over native ME-DLM across four inference budgets, with the largest gains under tight budgets. At one-eighth of the full scheduled forward budget, CaRR improves the six-metric average accuracy by 7.44 pp, including gains of 11.37 and 10.05 on MBPP and MBPP+.

Title: Suppressing the Shortcut Hidden Behind the Gains of VideoLLM Fine-Tuning

  • Authors: Youngwoo Shin, Jongsuk Kim, Junmo Kim
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25776
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25776

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is the stage in which Video Large Language Models (VideoLLMs) advance their capability to reason over video. However, the VideoLLM also learns to answer from textual regularities of its training data, a shortcut that bypasses the video. The regularities behind the shortcut are hard to define at the data level, and the shortcut forms regardless of training epoch, data size, or backbone. The shortcut is hidden behind a rise in accuracy within the training distribution, yet it is not required for the rise and even costs SFT the gain the video offers under distribution shift. We therefore propose Video-Evidence Training via Orthogonal projection (VETO), which intervenes on the fine-tuning update where the shortcut is written rather than on the data. VETO calibrates the subspace that the text alone occupies in the activations of the base model and projects the update onto its orthogonal complement. The VideoLLM is thus left to ground its answer in the full input, with the shortcut suppressed as it forms and never defined. Across seven VideoLLMs, VETO preserves the gain of SFT on the training distribution and suppresses the shortcut. On six benchmarks under shift it gains more than SFT and answers from the full input rather than the shortcut. Code is available at this https URL.

  • Abstract: Existing 3D generation methods synthesize visually compelling scenes from fixed inputs, yet struggle to continually expand and revise a 3D world as new observations arrive. Consequently, early generation errors can persist and accumulate despite new evidence. We propose World-delta, an incremental 3D scene generation framework combining uncertainty-aware persistent world modeling with delta-guided diffusion for continual scene expansion and correction. Our framework comprises three components: 1) Visual Evidence Encoding integrates geometric and semantic cues from incoming images into unified world tokens. 2) Observation-Calibrated World Modeling maintains a persistent latent world state that distinguishes observation-supported content from provisional completion hypotheses. New evidence drives selective world deltas to revise conflicting hypotheses while preserving well-supported content. 3) Incremental World Diffusion Generation uses delta-derived masks to restrict diffusion to regions requiring updates, freezing unchanged regions. Only affected 3D chunks are decoded and integrated into the existing scene. Together, these components enable continual world expansion and revision while reducing redundant generation and preserving scene consistency. Experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of World-delta in incremental 3D scene generation, observation-driven correction, scene consistency, and update efficiency over successive scene updates.

  • Abstract: Prosodic prominence can largely be predicted from the words of a sentence, but part of it depends on how the speaker reads it.   • Authors: Maxim Bolshim (1), Alexander Kugaevskikh (1) ((1) ITMO University, Saint Petersburg, Russia)
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25822

  • Keywords: segmentation
  • Abstract: We examine how well subspaces constructed from local curvature represent the change in the gradient update field after a finite step in semantic segmentation models. The local response is the action of the curvature operator on the actual input displacement. In a study of 50 images and two models, we measure the ratio of the loss increase attained in a fixed subspace to that attained in the full space. This ratio decreases as the search radius increases. On ten images from the same sample, we compare the one-dimensional subspace spanned by the initial gradient with two rank-16 subspaces for both models. The two equal-rank subspaces are assessed at the same initial states, using the same actual steps and measured vectors. Adding generalised Gauss-Newton directions to the initial gradient reduces the mean relative error in representing the field change from 0.922 to 0.874 for the first model and from 0.819 to 0.763 for the second. At the same rank, including the response direction for the initial gradient nearly eliminates the error in representing the local response. The mean error in representing the field change does not decrease. A decomposition with fixed pixel weights shows that the finite-step remainder is comparable in norm to the field change and makes the largest diagonal contribution to the squared projection error of the field after the step. Evaluation of a search subspace should measure how accurately it represents the field change and the local response.

  • Abstract: Low-rank plus sparse decompositions, such as robust PCA, typically model the low-rank component as dense and the sparse component as corruption. We propose signed biclique decomposition (SBD), a sparse low-rank decomposition for integer-weighted graphs that represents the structured component as a sum of sparse signed binary rank-one factors, together with a sparse signed residual. Empirically, SBD recovers block structure in noisy graphs, while its factorized representation enables efficient sparse matrix-matrix multiplication (SpMM).   • Abstract: Medical vision-language agents increasingly rely on segmentation tools, and recent work has focused on acquiring the right mask, often treating a good mask as the end point of tool use. But do these agents use the resulting evidence effectively? To answer this question, we need a benchmark that separates evidence use from segmentation quality, spans multiple medical image types, and allows the same mask to be presented in different ways. Existing medical VQA datasets do not jointly provide these properties. We therefore construct Mask-Evidence MedVQA, a 2.5K-example benchmark spanning four image types, where each question is paired with an answer-relevant ground-truth mask and multiple possible renderings. Our study reveals two limitations of direct tool use. First, evidence presentation matters. Different renderings of the same fixed mask can substantially change model behavior. Second, masks introduce an evidence trade-off. Highlighting a relevant region can expose diagnostic evidence the model previously missed, but the added visual cue can also induce incorrect associations. As a result, even a ground-truth mask can correct some answers while turning initially correct ones into incorrect ones. Together, these findings show that acquiring the right mask is not enough. An agent must also decide how to present the evidence and whether it should change the answer. We therefore introduce TUNE (Tool Use is Not Enough), an evidence-management harness that explicitly manages what evidence to acquire, how to present it, and whether to accept the resulting answer update. Across three public medical VQA benchmarks, evidence management improves the fine-tuned Qwen3.5-9B backbone's mean accuracy by 4.0 percentage points. Full TUNE improves over direct tool use by 9.1 points on average across seven benchmarks and achieves competitive results against existing medical VLMs and agents.

Title: TableRoute: Capability-Aware Routing for Scalable Table Question Answering

  • Authors: Yuxiang Wang, Junhao Gan, Jianzhong Qi
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25853
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25853
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25853]] TableRoute: Capability-Aware Routing for Scalable Table Question Answering(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25853)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Table question answering with large language models remains difficult to scale: tables range from compact to million-token sizes, while questions require precise grounding of columns, values, and computations. Existing methods often specialize in a fixed inference strategy, which is brittle across different table scales and model capabilities. Full-table reading is efficient for compact inputs but suffers from irrelevant context. Table reduction helps long inputs but depends on reliable evidence localization, while execution is required when tables exceed the model context window and remains sensitive to schema and value grounding errors. We propose TableRoute, a capability-aware routing framework with evidence-conditioned inference for scalable TableQA. TableRoute first generates validated question--table evidence and identifies the required operations, then routes each instance among these three strategies according to serialized table length and calibrated estimates of how reliably the base model handles these operations. The validated evidence supports the selected route by grounding columns, values, and conditions. Across WikiTQ, WTQ-L, BirdQA, and TableBench, TableRoute consistently improves accuracy through complementary routing and evidence conditioning across table scales, while reducing online token cost. Code will be made available upon publication.

  • Subjects: cs.LG, math.OC

  • Abstract: We propose X Hedging, an alternative to the Deep Hedging framework that addresses computational and explainability limitations of neural-network-based hedging strategies. X Hedging takes a step toward addressing these limitations by replacing neural networks with tree ensemble methods, specifically LightGBM, to estimate hedging strategies. Title: Verifiable Logic in Medical LLMs: A Critical Review of Agentic Clinical Reasoning Optimization and Neuro-Symbolic Grounding

  • Authors: Youssef ElNahas, Adel Ammar, Soumaya Chaffar, Wadii Boulila
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI, cs.CY, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25898
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25898

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Clinical large language models are usually judged by whether the final answer is right. Yet a model can reach a fluent, plausible output through invalid reasoning that static benchmarks do not detect, and confident hallucination, causally invalid inference, and spurious demographic shortcuts can pass conventional evaluation. This critical review covers four paradigms in turn: how clinical hallucination arises and why static metrics miss it; the agentic hallucination cascade, in which multi-agent and tool-augmented systems propagate and amplify early reasoning errors; process reward modeling as step-level supervision; and neuro-symbolic grounding, in which formal logic engines, ontology reasoners, and knowledge graphs place verifiable constraints on neural output. Within their encoded domains, systems with symbolic veto layers offer guarantees that hold relative to their encoded knowledge, and they report large gains on the safety measures they use. Process reward models remain bounded by the statistics of their training data. We propose a conceptual framework that maps each agentic failure mode to a symbolic intervention point, and we argue that clinical AI safety depends substantially on system architecture. Because current regulation emphasizes performance and does not require verification of reasoning steps, we argue that responsible certification should also audit reasoning trajectories alongside accuracy on static benchmarks.

Title: BlockJack: Cross-Tenant Redirection Fault Attacks on Paged KV Cache Address Translation in LLM Serving

  • Authors: Qifan Wang, Jeremy Boy, Jonas Sander, Yansong Gao, Hongsheng Hu, Ye Dong, Thomas Eisenbarth, David Oswald
  • Subjects: cs.CR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25945
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25945
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.25945]] BlockJack: Cross-Tenant Redirection Fault Attacks on Paged KV Cache Address Translation in LLM Serving(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25945)
  • Keywords: defense, attack, large language model
  • Abstract: Large language model (LLM) serving frameworks accelerate inference by retaining previously computed states in a key-value (KV) cache. Paged serving frameworks such as vLLM organize this cache through block tables analogous to OS page tables, which map each request's logical KV blocks to physical KV blocks, forming a software-managed address translation layer. Although fault attacks such as Rowhammer have exploited OS page tables to redirect memory accesses, prior fault attacks on LLMs have largely targeted the data plane, i.e., model weights or KV-cache contents, leaving KV-cache mapping unexplored. We present BlockJack (i.e., Block Hijack), the first KV cache address-translation fault attack in LLM inference. By corrupting block-table entries, BlockJack redirects a request's KV cache reads to blocks assigned to another tenant's request without modifying model parameters, prompts, or cached KV states. It achieves both confidentiality violation (victim information appears in attacker responses) and integrity violation (attacker-supplied context steers victim answers). We study paged KV cache mappings in vLLM and formally characterize when redirect targets are predictable. Across seven models spanning the Qwen, Llama, Gemma, and Phi families, a faulted mapping exposes victim context. For the integrity attack, we examine how topic proximity, intra-topic divergence, and model behavior shape redirected responses. On a 248-scene medical benchmark, the integrity attack causes up to 41.1% of responses to recommend less urgent care, while still appearing consistent with the victim's question. We demonstrate physical feasibility of BlockJack with Rowhammer and subsequently evaluate assignment validation as a defense. Following responsible disclosure, vLLM and Sonar confirmed the vulnerability and proposed patches that we reviewed and validated.

Title: Reinforcing Few-Step Diffusion Models via Shadow Posterior Matching

  • Authors: Jingqiao Xiu, Yicong Li
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.25998
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.25998

  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Few-step diffusion models are widely deployed for their efficiency. However, reinforcing them with black-box rewards, as is routine for multi-step models, faces two structural challenges. First, a few-step generator is a sampler rather than a score model, so its velocity is not the forward velocity of the generated distribution. Second, it must be anchored to the undistilled teacher, which shares no transitions with it. Existing few-step RL methods handle the latter with a marginal KL to the teacher, but each pays for the former in its reward credit. We propose Shadow Posterior Matching (SPM), which renoises generated samples into shadow inputs and obtains the credit from their shadow posterior. From the KL-regularized reward objective, we derive a regression loss that turns reward values into a reward direction while anchoring the generator's outputs to the teacher and aligning the shadow inputs with the deployed inputs. Applied to 4-step generators, SPM achieves higher rewards with less reward hacking than prior few-step RL methods.

Title: Does the Workspace Do the Work? A Causal Protocol for Verifying Global-Workspace Computation in a Language Model

  • Authors: Diaoxuan He, Linqian Wu
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI, q-bio.NC
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26018
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26018
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26018]] Does the Workspace Do the Work? A Causal Protocol for Verifying Global-Workspace Computation in a Language Model(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26018)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Global Workspace Theory (GWT), a leading theory of consciousness, holds that information becomes conscious when it is broadcast to specialized processes that use it. Building a workspace into a network does not reveal what the trained network computes with it: a module that reports an answer may have combined a broadcast message with its own knowledge, or merely forwarded an answer computed elsewhere, and task accuracy and ablation cannot tell these apart. We introduce a causal protocol that identifies who computes when modules communicate. Its central test transplants a message from another input, chosen so that ignoring, relaying and recombining it predict three different answers. On networks built to relay or to recombine, the test assigns the right computation in every case. Among trained workspace models it finds all three outcomes: one leaves its broadcast nearly unused, and of two models that differ only in their training data, one relays and the other recombines. In AFGW, a language model whose modules communicate only through a learned workspace, a transplanted message yields the answer that recombination predicts in 97% of cases, and its content acts by redirecting what the receiver retrieves from its own memory. The tests carry over to other communication designs and to a known interface of a pretrained Transformer, turning GWT's central claim into a testable property of trained models.

  • Abstract: Swarms of unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) detect Global Navigation Satellite System (GNSS) spoofing by checking the drones' reported positions against their known formation. We show that such an attack still leaves a trace below the position level. Because the transmitters are close, each drone sees them under a slightly different angle, and this parallax bends the signal arrival times across a 90 m swarm by 20-40 cm for a transmitter at 5 km, whatever delays the attacker applies. We propose a likelihood ratio test that reads this trace from the pseudoranges and Doppler every drone already measures, with all clocks and attacker delays unknown and no extra antennas. Title: The Delayed Societal Cost of Unfairness

  • Authors: Giovani Valdrighi, Isabel Valera, Marcos Medeiros Raimundo
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26030
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26030
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26030]] The Delayed Societal Cost of Unfairness(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26030)
  • Keywords: fair
  • Abstract: Fair machine learning typically evaluates the societal costs of a decision policy -- the loss incurred by the decision-maker (DM) and the unfair distribution of costs among demographic groups of decision-subjects (DS) -- on static data, where reducing one often comes at the expense of the other. Yet decisions change the populations on which they act, and prior work has shown that fairness interventions designed on static data may fail to benefit, or even harm, the disadvantaged group over time. We investigate a complementary question: When can a policy that is Pareto-optimal on pre-deployment data be outperformed by a fairer policy once each is evaluated on the population it induces, reducing costs for one stakeholder without increasing them for the other? In a setting where qualification scores evolve in response to binary decisions and their outcomes, we show that under Rawlsian fairness, which never lowers any group's acceptance rate, placing sufficiently high weight on fairness reduces societal costs whenever accepting disadvantaged DS at the DM's break-even point leaves them in a state less costly to the DM than rejecting them. Under egalitarian fairness, which may lower the advantaged group's acceptance rate, this additionally requires the reduction in DM loss on the disadvantaged group to outweigh the increase on the advantaged group. Under both fairness notions, however, when DM loss is accuracy-based, improvements in the post-deployment population may arise from declining DS qualification, revealing that gains for the DM do not necessarily translate into better outcomes for DS. Our results show that policy-induced population dynamics can overturn apparent performance-fairness trade-offs, allowing fairer policies to reduce the delayed societal costs of decision-making.

Title: PaveSplat: Resolution-Robust Feed-Forward Gaussian Splatting with Residually Corrected Surface-Aligned Covariances

  • Authors: Janghun Kim, Hyun-Cheol Kim, Eunbin An, Myeonghun Oh, Hyon-Gon Choo
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26045
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26045
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26045]] PaveSplat: Resolution-Robust Feed-Forward Gaussian Splatting with Residually Corrected Surface-Aligned Covariances(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26045)
  • Keywords: robust
  • Abstract: We present PaveSplat, a feed-forward Gaussian splatting method that adds a learned residual to the analytic surface-aligned covariance, keeping surfels paving the visible surface across rendering resolutions. Pixel-aligned feed-forward Gaussian splatting predicts one primitive per pixel from a few images in a single pass, and its rendering quality depends on how well these primitives cover the surface. Recent surfel-based methods compute the surface-aligned covariance analytically from predicted depth. However, this covariance has no way to correct the surfel orientation where the depth-derived frame is ill-posed. The surfels then cover the surface loosely; at the training resolution each surfel's one-pixel minimum footprint covers the gaps, but they show up as holes when the same prediction is re-rendered at a higher resolution. To address this, we keep the analytic covariance as an initialization, with its axes aligned to the surface tangents, and add an identity-initialized unit-quaternion residual that reorients a surfel only where this improves rendering, turning surfels that the analytic frame mis-aims back onto the surface. On RealEstate10K, PaveSplat achieves the best PSNR, SSIM, and LPIPS among single-Gaussian-per-pixel methods at every scale (1x-8x). The native-resolution gain is small; the benefit appears when the same prediction is rendered at higher display resolutions without re-running the network. On zero-shot DL3DV, it leads PSNR within this group at every scale and ranks first or second on SSIM/LPIPS. A coverage-hole analysis shows fewer uncovered pixels than both baselines under high-resolution re-rendering.

Title: Complete-SVD: A Functional Norm Approach to Low-Rank LLM Compression

  • Authors: Alper Kalle, Theo Rudkiewicz, Mohamed-Oumar Ouerfelli, Mohamed Tamaazousti
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26059
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26059
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26059]] Complete-SVD: A Functional Norm Approach to Low-Rank LLM Compression(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26059)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Post-training low-rank compression of neural networks must not only approximate the original weights but also preserve the model's input-output behavior. Recent output-aware methods measure the compression error beyond the compressed layer, at the model output, weighting logit changes by the observed next token or by the model's predicted probabilities. We propose Complete-SVD, a method for large language models that minimizes the $L_2$ distance between the logits of the original and compressed models, weighting all logit changes equally. Linearizing the network around the original weights, together with a Kronecker-factored approximation, reduces this objective to a weighted SVD with a closed-form solution. Its output-side factor is intractable to compute; we estimate it without bias from random probes, at a cost of $K$ backward passes per sequence, independent of vocabulary size and sequence length. At matched settings with uniform ranks and WikiText-2 calibration, Complete-SVD achieves the lowest C4 and PTB perplexity in every setting, and at ratio 0.7 under LEMS rank allocation. Replacing the $L_2$ distance by the Kullback-Leibler divergence (KL-SVD), with the same estimation framework, lowers calibration perplexity in most settings and raises downstream accuracy, but raises cross-corpus perplexity. Complete-SVD also improves IFEval instruction-following accuracy over the strongest prior output-aware methods throughout.

Title: Iterative Scarcity-Guided Exploration: Bootstrapping Generative Auto-bidding from Narrow Support

  • Abstract: Auto-bidding is a core algorithmic component in online advertising auctions. Extensive experiments on the industrial AuctionNet benchmark demonstrate that ISGE, starting from cold-start datasets, effectively surpasses the performance of the Full-Dataset baseline within three iterations. Title: AltRA-Test: An Alternative Annotator Test for LLM Judges Without Shared Annotators

  • Authors: Nagham Omar, Rotem Dror, Almog Gueta, Avigdor Gal, Roi Reichart, Nitay Calderon
  • Subjects: cs.CL, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26073
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26073
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26073]] AltRA-Test: An Alternative Annotator Test for LLM Judges Without Shared Annotators(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26073)
  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as judges and graders to replace human annotators, raising the question of when such replacement is statistically justified. To support such decisions, the Alternative Annotator Test (Alt-Test) was introduced for dense settings where all items are annotated by the same annotators. However, in many large-scale annotation settings, items are annotated by different subsets of annotators. We therefore propose the Alternative Annotator Test with Resampled Annotations (AltRA-Test), a statistical test that does not require that all items are annotated by the same annotators. AltRA-Test uses a bootstrap that resamples items, draws k human annotations for each item, and substitutes one of them with an LLM annotation. This produces paired all-human and mixed human-LLM panels. A replacement is supported when the inter-annotator agreement (IAA) of the mixed panel is statistically non-inferior to the IAA of the all-human panel, i.e., when the LLM preserves the signal that the human annotators share. We establish that AltRA-Test is a consistent test of asymptotic level alpha. We then evaluate it through simulations and real-world experiments in which we test 18 LLMs and 5 prompting techniques on 20 datasets across text, image, audio, and video. Across these evaluations, about 100 items often suffice for the test. In simulations, where we know whether the LLM is a better or a worse annotator than the humans, AltRA-Test makes more correct replacement decisions than existing baselines. In real-world data experiments, LLMs pass the test in about half of the setups, depending mainly on the task. Since replacement is not always justified, we encourage researchers to test it for their task.

  • Subjects: cs.CL

  • Abstract: LLMs are increasingly used as automated evaluators to reduce the cost and time required for human annotation. However, the role of scoring-scheme design in the reliability of LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation remains underexplored. To address this problem, we introduce numerical scoring topology, which characterizes the structural design of a scoring scheme in terms of its scale width, granularity, offset, symmetry, and boundary placement. Building on this definition, we conduct the first systematic study of how numerical scoring topology influences zero-shot LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation across diverse benchmarks and judge models. Our results show that numerical scoring topology systematically affects judge scores, calibration, and robustness, particularly in judgment-intensive evaluations.   • Keywords: attack, fair
  • Abstract: Fair transaction ordering protocols aim to mitigate front-running using replica-local arrival orders, but prior work often assumes a single submission origin for each responsive transaction. We identify causal overtaking, in which corrupted replicas observe a target and submit identical signed copies of a responsive transaction through multiple client entries. The copies' first arrivals at different replicas can collectively provide ordering support that no individual path achieves, allowing the response to execute before its target. We formalize a class of receipt-order protocols, including Themis, characterize the timing and network conditions under which the attack is feasible, and show that delayed gossip does not rule it out. Finally, we evaluate the attack and mitigation on Themis through simulations driven by AWS and WonderProxy latency measurements.

  • Abstract: Network topology influences decentralized federated learning through information mixing and the relative contribution of individual nodes.   • Copy Paste: [[2611.26178]] FlexVSR: Continuous-Scale Super-Resolution for Streaming Video Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26178)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer, generative
  • Abstract: Real-time streaming video generation has gained rapid momentum in live content creation and interactive media. Deploying such systems in practice requires flexible scaling factors to accommodate diverse source resolutions, target displays, and computational budgets. However, extending fixed-scale generative video super-resolution (VSR) to varying target scales poses two challenges: downsampling from a larger fixed scale incurs redundant cost, while re-optimizing the diffusion transformer (DiT) is costly and alters the shared generative prior. To address both, we propose FlexVSR, a decoupled framework for continuous-scale streaming generative VSR with supervision at only a few scales. To this end, FlexVSR introduces ReLSA (Refined Latent Scale Adaptation), a lightweight latent adapter that constructs and refines target-grid latents before they enter the DiT, enabling a shared frozen VSR prior to generalize across continuous scales with only 0.274M trainable parameters and an adaptation cost of 7.3 H100 GPU-hours, while introducing no additional temporal dependencies and preserving the native causal streaming protocol. Extensive experiments demonstrate competitive or superior reconstruction and temporal quality, lower latency than most native-scale streaming baselines and offline methods, and effective generalization to unseen continuous scales, filling the continuous-scale gap in real-time streaming generative VSR.

Title: Invertible Transport Between Sample-Only Distributions via Projected Characteristic Functions

  • Authors: Juan Gutiérrez, Victor Gutiérrez-García, José Luis Blanco-Murillo
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26187
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26187
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26187]] Invertible Transport Between Sample-Only Distributions via Projected Characteristic Functions(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26187)
  • Keywords: generative
  • Abstract: Many useful latent distributions can be sampled but do not have a tractable density. Such distributions may be represented by empirical collections of real-world observations or generated by scientific simulators and black-box generative models. These sample-only priors cannot be used directly in likelihood-based normalizing flows, which require evaluating the prior density. We propose Projected Characteristic-Function matching (PCF), a density-free framework for learning an invertible map between a data distribution and a prior specified only through samples. Our method aligns distributions using projected empirical characteristic functions, eliminating the need for density estimation, adversarial training, or paired observations. To address the non-uniqueness of the transport map, we introduce an optional transport regularizer that selects lower-displacement transformations while preserving endpoint alignment. We benchmark PCF on controlled transports, sample-efficiency studies, and high-dimensional alignment tasks. PCF matches the accuracy of kernel MMD on the controlled benchmark and on raw-pixel MNIST--USPS at a cost linear in the batch size, and it is more sample-efficient, achieving lower endpoint error than MMD in 13 of 15 paired runs across library sizes. By avoiding pairwise kernel evaluation, it reduces discrepancy memory by 29.8% and trains at batch sizes where kernel MMD exceeds GPU memory. These results show that PCF provides accurate and efficient invertible transport, particularly in sample- and memory-limited settings.

Title: Learn, Freeze, and Transfer an Activation Function Across Architectures

  • Authors: Arun Prakash A
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26201
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26201
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26201]] Learn, Freeze, and Transfer an Activation Function Across Architectures(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26201)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Transfer learning typically reuses knowledge acquired by a source network through its learned weights. We investigate whether a learned activation function can also be transferred and remain useful in networks trained from scratch. We study this complementary form of transfer through a learn-freeze-transfer protocol, in which the activation is optimized jointly with a source network in a single training run, and is then frozen and reused in target networks trained from scratch. A pointwise activation with globally shared parameters is independent of layer dimensions and can therefore be incorporated into networks of different architectures trained from scratch. When the learning rate and weight decay are tuned separately for each activation under the same search budget, Frozen FAct still ranks first in 11 of the 16 settings, second in four, and fourth in one. These results provide empirical evidence that a learned activation function can retain its usefulness when transferred across vision architectures, independently of the weights with which it was learned.

  • Abstract: Source-free domain adaptation (SFDA) adapts a pretrained source model to unlabeled target domains without source data access, yet two critical failure modes remain unaddressed. First, attention drift: source-learned attention maps shift toward spurious or background features under distribution shift, destabilizing feature representations. Second, domain-gap-agnostic initialization: applying a single fixed source model uniformly across all target domains ignores domain gap severity, causing premature overfitting to noisy pseudo-labels under large shifts. The Domain-Gap Aware Source Model Selection (DGASS) module estimates domain gap severity via target prediction entropy and selects the source model variant whose confidence level best matches the estimated gap, reducing negative transfer and stabilizing pseudo-label quality. Crucially, CAFC transfers beyond SFDA: applied as a lightweight regularizer to standard domain generalization objectives without target data. Extensive experiments across SFDA and DG benchmarks demonstrate that our method consistently improves over recent baselines, demonstrating the effectiveness and generality of the proposed framework.
