Title: dLoop: Looped Diffusion Language Models via Recurrent Latent Refinement

  • Authors: Handa Li, Pengxiang Li, Mingyu Cao, Xilu Wang, Li Shen, Da Li, Lu Yin
  • Subjects: cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26275
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26275
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26275]] dLoop: Looped Diffusion Language Models via Recurrent Latent Refinement(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26275)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) support parallel generation, yet they often require many denoising steps because only a fraction of token predictions are sufficiently reliable to be committed at each step. Existing acceleration methods primarily optimize confidence learning or commitment policies. However, these approaches operate on the outputs of a single denoising forward pass, in which information from the prompt, previously committed tokens, and uncommitted positions may not yet be fully integrated. Consequently, some predictions remain under-refined when the commitment decision is made, and modifying the commitment policy alone cannot improve their underlying representations. We introduce dLoop, which restructures a pretrained diffusion language model into a Prelude-Loop-Coda architecture and recurrently applies a parameter-shared intermediate block to the same partially masked state. Rather than advancing the denoising process, each additional loop provides further latent computation, allowing information distributed across uncommitted positions to continue interacting and become more fully integrated before commitment. Experiments on GSM8K, MATH, MBPP, and HumanEval show that dLoop achieves the highest or tied-highest accuracy on every benchmark. With tokens per forward pass (TPF) adjusted for loop computation, it outperforms Fast-dLLM in both accuracy and TPF on all four benchmarks and dParallel on three, and is more accurate than d3LLM on all four, while d3LLM attains higher TPF; no compared method surpasses dLoop in both. Mechanistic analyses show that extra loops mainly help on slow, unstable trajectories and that updates at uncommitted positions drive changes in token decisions and later generation. Pre-commitment latent computation thus makes predictions more reliable, offering a route to DLM acceleration distinct from forcing confidence through the training loss.

Title: Online Temporal Rotations for 2 Bit KV-Cache Quantization in Video Diffusion Transformers

  • Authors: Ryan Lucas, Petros Prastakos, Rahul Mazumder
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26334
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26334
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26334]] Online Temporal Rotations for 2 Bit KV-Cache Quantization in Video Diffusion Transformers(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26334)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Video diffusion transformers reuse key--value (KV) caches across denoising steps, which quickly becomes a bottleneck for long video generation. We find that quantizing temporally distant cache vectors leads to a large range and high quantization error, but that nearby video tokens in the KV-cache share a persistent pattern per channel. These patterns also vary across videos, motivating rotations that are adapted to each video's unique cache. We propose Temporal Rotation Quantization (TRQ), a method that rotates frames in the cache that are adjacent in time before quantization. For each block of temporally adjacent cache vectors, TRQ fits an orthogonal transform that maps the block's per-channel means to a vector that is close to a constant across channels. To keep the quantization format lightweight, we parameterize this rotation as a tree of $2 \times 2$ planar rotations, each stored with only two bits, followed by a fixed Hadamard transform shared across all blocks. The rotations are fit without gradients or changes to model weights, and we prove that a simple greedy fit is globally optimal for our 'mean-flattening' objective. The transforms are fit online for each new video, and we release fused Triton kernels to make this process efficient at inference. At 2-bit precision and matched compression, TRQ improves VBench subject consistency over the state-of-the-art method QVG from 90.38 to 92.02 on LongCat-Video-13B and from 86.33 to 87.61 on HY-WorldPlay-8B, as well as improving other metrics.

Title: OffBeat: Multicoset Sampling for 4D Capture Beyond the Camera Frame Rate

  • Authors: Chi-Wei Lee, An-Kai Chen, Cheng-Han Chiang, Yi-Chun Chang, Po-Chih Kuo
  • Subjects: cs.CV, eess.IV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26347
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26347
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26347]] OffBeat: Multicoset Sampling for 4D Capture Beyond the Camera Frame Rate(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26347)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: For 4D reconstruction, a synchronized multi-camera rig records all views at the same timestamps and misses the motion between them. Staggered triggers spread the same frames over more timestamps. However, when the cameras along each row fire one after another, as in 4DSloMo's schedule on the N3DV rig, the row acts as one moving camera that confounds depth with motion. We introduce OffBeat, which models a staggered rig as a spatially distributed multicoset sampler: shifted copies of one sampling grid. From the calibration alone, OffBeat derives camera groups that fire together and their firing order. Since one group records each timestamp, a short-lived primitive can fit an error its cameras share, such as a color offset, and flicker in novel views. We show that a primitive's opacity over time weights all groups equally, for every number of groups, only if it lasts at least one camera frame interval. On Spacetime Gaussians, a minimum lifetime approximates this bound, and a closed-form smoothed box of that length satisfies it exactly, with less flicker than a Gaussian in its place at nearly equal PSNR. In simulated staggered capture on N3DV, OffBeat outperforms the full 4DSloMo pipeline with video diffusion on every test frame, by 3.04 dB in mean dynamic-region PSNR. Between the first and last synchronized frame, it outperforms the best synchronized method of each scene, with or without frame interpolation, by 1.98 dB in mean dynamic-region PSNR, while synchronized capture is better at its own frames. On PanopticSports, in this frame range, OffBeat is comparable in PSNR to synchronized capture with interpolation. On every N3DV scene, replacing 4DSloMo's schedule with ours raises the dynamic-region PSNR of Spacetime Gaussians, 4DSloMo without diffusion, and our reconstruction. Multi-camera rigs could follow faster motion at the same frame count by changing only when each camera fires.

Title: Learning from Context: Leave-One-Out Prediction in Jump Diffusions

  • Authors: Christian Wald
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26359
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26359
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26359]] Learning from Context: Leave-One-Out Prediction in Jump Diffusions(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26359)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Continuous diffusion models generate discrete data through stochastic dynamics in an embedding space. We consider jump diffusions that additionally reset coordinates to embeddings of categorical values. A coordinate's own corrupted state can make prediction easy, potentially reducing the pressure to learn dependencies between coordinates. We study leave-one-out (LOO) prediction, in which each coordinate is predicted from the corrupted states of the other coordinates. We show that, under stated assumptions, jump diffusions with complete coordinate resets and state-independent clocks preserve the prescribed marginals when reset destinations use exact LOO probabilities and the drift uses the full endpoint posterior. We further show that this drift can be constructed from LOO probabilities by analytic likelihood completion without additional network evaluations. We evaluate unrestricted, LOO, and completed predictions on Text8 and LM1B text generation and ZINC250k molecular graph generation. LOO-based variants improve key generation metrics over matched unrestricted controls on all three datasets. Controlled interventions assess reliance on local inputs and context, and we analyze the effect of analytic likelihood completion.

Title: HiSA: Sparsifying the Hidden Dimension of RoPE Attention in LLMs

  • Authors: Ambroise Borbely, Mathieu Salzmann, Olivier Verscheure
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.CL
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26363

  • Keywords: large language model
  • Abstract: Attention in large language models (LLMs) scores every query against every key with a full dot product. We investigate whether this dot product must be computed completely for attention to be effective. We propose Hidden Sparse Attention (HiSA), which keeps the query and key projections intact but only computes a partial dot product, following a static, parameter-free rule set by the token positions: attention becomes sparse in the hidden (feature) dimension, an axis much less explored than token sparsity, rather than over tokens as in almost all sparse attention methods. By training LLMs from scratch with HiSA, we find that this partial dot product does not hurt but improves performance compared with the complete product while computing only about a third of the query-key products. During inference HiSA improves decoding speed and shrinks memory footprint. We also extend HiSA to FlashAttention-3 kernels for training and inference. Title: FEA solver integration of a composite World Model with a Spiking Neural Network memory Block for computationally efficient modelling of defects and variabilities

  • Authors: Bassam El Said, Adam Sobey
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26419
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26419
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26419]] FEA solver integration of a composite World Model with a Spiking Neural Network memory Block for computationally efficient modelling of defects and variabilities(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26419)
  • Keywords: transformer
  • Abstract: Each fibre-reinforced composite structure has a unique material architecture. The manufacturing methods used and the presence of defects lead to local variations which require material-scale models that can resolve the response in real time at every location in the structure. Concurrent multi-scale schemes provide this capability, but at a prohibitive computational cost for large structures as the number of integration points and time increments scales with size. Surrogates have been studied extensively as replacements for material-scale models to mitigate these computational limitations. A surrogate must represent an arbitrary local architecture including defects, track the irreversible material state over an unknown number of increments, remain stable when driven recursively by its own predictions, and fit within the memory available at every integration point. Here, a Composites World Model based on a Multi-Modal Spiking Transformer architecture is designed to meet these requirements. The proposed architecture combines a material encoder, a Spiking Neural Network memory block that tracks the long-term internal state of the material, and transformer blocks that learn the short-term applied strain state. The system is trained in three stages: material architecture encoding, base training on short time windows, and recursive fine-tuning with scheduled sampling to suppress cumulative error drift. The trained model is disassembled at inference and coupled to a non-linear FE solver, enabling structural scale simulation of composites containing embedded defects. Benchmarks against experimental results for IM7/8552 laminates under tension, compression and transverse shear, for pristine and wrinkled composites, show that the SNN memory block successfully tracks the material internal state and results in prediction errors between 0.4% and 4.8%.

  • Abstract: Recent advances in image restoration have greatly improved global image fidelity, producing visually plausible results across most regions of an image. Although these errors typically affect only a small portion of the image, they often dominate the overall perceptual quality of the result. In this work, we propose Failure-Aware Image Restoration (FAIR) to address the "last-mile" problem in image restoration. Our method complements existing restoration pipelines by shifting the emphasis from global fidelity to local perceptual acceptability. Title: Direct3D-V3: Latent-Free Diffusion Transformers in the Native Voxel Domain

  • Authors: Shuang Wu, Jiachen Qian, Youtian Lin, Feihu Zhang, Philip Torr, Yao Yao, Xun Cao

  • Abstract: Native 3D generation has largely been built around latent diffusion, with recent advances concentrating on stronger variational autoencoders (VAE) and increasingly elaborate latent representations, from vecset and triplanes to structured sparse voxels. Despite their architectural differences, these methods all share the same premise: geometry is first compressed into a latent space and only then modeled by the generator. We take a different route and propose Direct3D-V3, a latent-free diffusion transformer (DiT) framework that discards the VAE altogether and operates in the native voxel domain, directly modeling raw SDF voxel grids during both training and sampling. To improve alignment between the conditioning image and generated geometry, Direct3D-V3 also predicts the camera parameters of the conditioning image as an auxiliary task, encouraging the DiT to learn the mapping from image observations to 3D shape. This latent-free design removes the reconstruction bottleneck, brings training under an objective in the native voxel domain, and yields meshes with substantially richer local detail. The predicted camera and native SDF voxel output further make Direct3D-V3 extensible to generative reconstruction, where diffusion is interleaved with normal-guided refinement to improve alignment with the observed view while the generative prior completes unseen regions.   • Authors: Tara Sowrirajan, Yanqiao Wang
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.IR
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26521
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26521

  • Keywords: robust, transformer
  • Abstract: In our experiments on long-horizon customer-behavior forecasting, a longer observation window outweighed the observed effects of more data, more capacity and newer architectures. The window is the weeks of history a model sees before the week it forecasts from. Going from an 8- to a 16-week window adds a raw +0.070 to 0.107 log-space $R^2$ in every family at 1M. Matched test cohorts, which score the same customer-weeks at every window, show this raw comparison understates the gain in every family they cover. The direction holds on five public datasets, four spanning over a year (ridge and our transformer on all five). On our internal data, longer windows admit up to 10% more and colder training customers, which matching does not fix. Our design does not separate window length from calendar coverage, because every test anchor (the week a forecast is made from) falls in one eight-week band. On training-period anchors, the weekly ridge probe's gain persists outside the holiday season, keeping about 72% of it. We therefore report a robust positive association with longer windows and do not identify the magnitude of a calendar-independent history effect. Of four internally registered predictions, the only fully blind one failed. An activity-mixture model predicted +0.014 from 24 to 32 weeks; the weekly-aggregate probe returned -0.0004. For the sequence families, the other three levers are about an order of magnitude smaller, bounded and unresolved. Their observed effects are at most 0.006 between the nominal 1M and 10M tiers and 0.0052 among five sequence families at 16 weeks with clocks as built, both single-seed on the full test split, and 0.006 for capacity on three-seed means. These are observed intervals, not equivalence bounds. Our claim is the order-of-magnitude contrast between the window and those bounds. A team building on this class of data should start by lengthening the window.

Title: Backtracking Agent Applications Security Incidents

  • Authors: Yicheng Cai, Taegyu Kim, Peng Liu
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26525
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26525
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26525]] Backtracking Agent Applications Security Incidents(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26525)
  • Keywords: security, attack, fair
  • Abstract: Agent applications are increasingly deployed, yet agent-specific attacks pose real threats to them. Upon a security incident, organizations backtrack to identify the CEP (compromise entry point), remediate the damage, and prevent its recurrence. The main artifacts available for backtracking are trajectory logs, which record agent functionalities, and source code; app(lication)-specific function call logs are often fairly limited. However, trajectory logs are at the agent functionality abstraction layer, while the CEP candidates are function calls at the app code execution layer. Due to this semantic gap, the analyst needs to manually inspect the relevant code for each CEP candidate to infer its corresponding agent functionality. In this work, we present Origin, a backtracking tool for the analyst to submit a small number of queries to identify CEPs that existing tools cannot provide. Our approach poses two challenges: (1) define the intermediate abstraction that captures the mapping relationships between the two semantic layers; (2) determine the region-to-variable mapping for constructing the cross-layer graph defined in the intermediate abstraction. Each region is a specific group of agent functionalities. Origin addresses the challenges by (1) the cross-layer dependency graph (CLDG) that connects region-inferred functions at the semantic level to the variables in the low-level taint propagation subgraph, plus the associated query language, and (2) the region inference algorithm. Case studies on five popular agent applications across eight attack incidents show that an analyst using Origin correctly identifies all the CEPs.

Title: Replication of Agentic Security Events: Audit and Investigation (ROGUE-AI)

  • Authors: Mengxiao Wang, Mahbub Alam, Kalyan Nakka, Nitesh Saxena
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.MA
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26553

  • Abstract: Agents based on large language models (LLMs) can now delete files and send payments on behalf of their users. Incident catalogs collect these reports, and benchmarks record whether agents fail, but neither replicates an incident nor tests the cause its report might identify. In this paper, we present ROGUE-AI (Replication of Agentic Security Events: Audit and Investigation), a multi-agent framework that closes the above gap and rebuilds each reported incident offline together with a matched control that lacks only the blamed cause, and runs agents on both. Our results suggest that ROGUE-AI is able to reproduce the reported harm in 89% of its 108 attempted replications. Of the 72 incidents with real-world harm, 62 had no underlying attacker, and in these, agents did what their task asked for but missed an ordinary hazard, such as a database shared with the live site. Replications without an attacker caused more harm than replications of prompt injection for all five models. Defenses against prompt injection look for injected instructions, but these replications contain none. Further, a warning after each tool output that names the hazard and its consequence lowered harm for all five models, whereas a note of the same length that does not mention the hazard left most of the harm in place. Built from real incidents, the ROGUE-AI replications form a reusable audit framework that vendors can rerun before each new release.

Generative engine optimization (GEO) services sell visibility in these answers by publishing promotional articles on user-generated-content (UGC) platforms for paying clients. Such promotion raises concerns in medical and financial domains, where misleading advice can cause harm. To study the severity of this problem, we measure one such paid-content operation across three major Chinese UGC platforms, uncovering 1,985 accounts associated with the network comprising 466,357 inferred promotional placements. We find that the network promotes products across diverse categories through a growing pool of accounts, conducting coordinated campaigns spanning months across multiple platforms while evolving its listicle content over time. Across nine generative engines from both the US and China, we find that 4.5% to 20% of retrievable cited pages are attributable to the network, depending on how strictly attribution is drawn. On eight of these engines, its clients are named in 52% of answers, against 68% for category leaders. Our work demonstrates the effectiveness of UGC GEO placement at influencing generative engine outcomes, and reveals content-level defenses as insufficient against coordinated UGC placements, where account-level and platform-level signals are necessary for proper defense.

Title: Active Feature Acquisition by Distilling an Oracle Teacher

  • Authors: Yang Meng, Zixin Ding, Robert L. Grossman, Yuxin Chen
  • Subjects: cs.LG, cs.AI
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26598
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26598
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26598]] Active Feature Acquisition by Distilling an Oracle Teacher(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26598)
  • Keywords: privacy
  • Abstract: Acquiring every feature of an instance is often costly: a measurement can carry a monetary price, a time delay, or a privacy cost. Active Feature Acquisition (AFA) addresses this by learning a policy that decides, per instance, which features to acquire under a budget. Existing AFA methods train this policy by optimizing a self-constructed utility target rather than distilling an explicit teacher. We construct this teacher explicitly. At training time the label and the unobserved feature values are available, and together they determine the teacher in closed form: an oracle that ranks every candidate by its effect on the downstream predictor's error. OracleImitator distills this ranking into a deployable policy by soft-target imitation. Across multimodal medical diagnosis and the AFABench benchmark, OracleImitator matches or outperforms existing AFA methods across the budget range, recovering 90% of the attainable accuracy gain at under a quarter of the acquisition cost. Because the teacher is an explicit algorithmic object rather than an implicit optimization target, we can also measure directly how much of its ranking is recoverable by a policy restricted to what is visible at deployment.

Title: SoK: Assessing the Evolution of LLM Capability in Software Vulnerability Research

  • Authors: Amine Lbath, Manan Suri, Aurelien Delaitre, Vadim Okun, Massih-Reza Amini, Dinesh Manocha
  • Subjects: cs.CR, cs.AI, cs.SE
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26601
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26601
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26601]] SoK: Assessing the Evolution of LLM Capability in Software Vulnerability Research(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26601)
  • Keywords: security, attack
  • Abstract: LLM-based agents are becoming increasingly capable at discovering, validating and patching vulnerabilities in real software, and vendors and researchers report that this is already changing how vulnerabilities are found and fixed. These claims, however, are scattered across papers, vendor reports and disclosures and are rarely comparable. We introduce a framework that rates these capabilities on an autonomy scale modeled on the levels of driving automation, and qualifies each claim by its correctness, its availability and the strength of its evidence. We use this framework to systematize the published evidence on LLM capability in vulnerability research. We find that agents discovered, validated, and patched vulnerabilities in real-world software without human intervention before the restricted frontier models of 2026, and that open-weight models subsequently attained comparable autonomy for discovery and validation. We then measure the consequences of evolving LLM security capabilities in a decade of public vulnerability records, advisories and exploitation data. In 2026 the number of disclosed vulnerabilities more than doubled, with an increasing share rated critical or high, and severe bugs that had survived a decade or more in widely used code were reported several times more often than before. Yet the time between the stages of a vulnerability's life, from report to fix and from disclosure to attack, has not shifted noticeably so far. We grade the mitigations proposed in the literature with an explicit rubric and draw guidance for each group of stakeholders from the best-supported ones.

Title: Bio-PM-Guided Synthetic IMU Generation

  • Authors: Deepali Singh, Deepesh Parpani, Aviral Gupta
  • Subjects: cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26607
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26607
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26607]] Bio-PM-Guided Synthetic IMU Generation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26607)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Collecting and labeling inertial measurement unit (IMU) data for human activity recognition (HAR) is expensive, and models trained on one group of subjects often fail to generalize to unseen individuals. Even with balanced data, classifiers struggle to distinguish dynamically similar activity classes and benefit from targeted augmentation. We explore whether synthetic IMU data generated in the representation space of a pretrained biosignal encoder, Bio-PM \cite{b1}, can serve as a practical augmentation source. Rather than synthesizing raw signals, we leverage Bio-PM's frozen encoder to convert 10-second IMU windows into token embedding sequences and train class-conditional decoders in this space: (1) an autoregressive transformer decoder that predicts tokens sequentially, and (2) a diffusion decoder that learns the token distribution through a denoising objective. We analyze generation realism through distributional metrics and embedding-space visualizations, evaluate downstream utility through leave-one-subject-out HAR classification, and present a PatchDecoder for approximate raw waveform reconstruction. Our diffusion decoder achieves a Train-on-Synthetic-Test-on-Real (TSTR) macro-F1 of 0.68, demonstrating that generated tokens are realistic enough to train a classifier that transfers to real data. Combining synthetic-token augmentation with a 320-dimensional gravity-aware feature representation raises LOSO macro-F1 from 0.729 to 0.809 over a real-only baseline across 51 subjects on the WISDM benchmark, with statistically significant gains concentrated in the most challenging activity classes. These results demonstrate that high-fidelity generation in a pretrained encoder's representation space is achievable and practically useful for cross-subject HAR augmentation.

Title: One Prior, Two Dynamics: Repurposing Video Diffusion Transformers for Self-Supervised Latent Action Learning

  • Authors: Yuxin Jiang, Wenqiang Sun, Jiaqi Li, Yuchao Gu, Lvmin Zhang, Ivor Tsang, Mike Zheng Shou
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.AI, cs.LG, cs.RO
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26617
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26617
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26617]] One Prior, Two Dynamics: Repurposing Video Diffusion Transformers for Self-Supervised Latent Action Learning(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26617)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer, generative
  • Abstract: Videos reveal how the world changes, but rarely the actions that cause those changes. Latent action learning recovers such supervision from visual transitions, yet often learns the underlying dynamics largely from scratch. We ask whether a pretrained video generator can instead provide the prior for both inverse and forward dynamics. We introduce O$^{2}$LaF, built on a simple principle: one prior, two dynamics. We initialize both models from the same pretrained video DiT and adapt them to their distinct roles: a feed-forward transition encoder with learnable action queries for inverse dynamics, and a causal, action-conditioned video generator for forward dynamics. By controlling information access and optimizing both dynamics end-to-end through a compact bottleneck, O$^{2}$LaF learns transferable actions from unlabeled videos. Across navigation and robotic manipulation, O$^{2}$LaF sets a new state of the art in few-shot action probing and enables stronger zero-shot action transfer across visual contexts and embodiments. Beyond generative video priors, we further extend our framework to predictive representation priors such as V-JEPA2, achieving competitive performance. Our findings suggest a broader paradigm for latent-action learning: repurposing pretrained video models to discover transferable actions and predict their consequences.

Title: RL Post-Training in Diffusion Transformers Is Activation Steering

  • Authors: Tuna Han Salih Meral, Kaan Oktay, Alec Helbling, Atakan Kara, Duen Horng Chau, Pinar Yanardag
  • Subjects: cs.CV, cs.LG
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26627
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26627
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26627]] RL Post-Training in Diffusion Transformers Is Activation Steering(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26627)
  • Keywords: diffusion, transformer
  • Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training substantially improves the visual quality, text rendering, and compositional accuracy of diffusion transformers. Because capabilities such as rendering requested text or composing multiple objects are inherently prompt-dependent, a natural hypothesis is that improving them requires learning complex, input-dependent transformations. To isolate how much of these gains an input-independent shift explains, we probe each RL-tuned model with a deliberately simple parameterization: a fixed set of steering vectors added to the block outputs of the frozen base model. On SD3.5-M, these vectors hold 65x fewer parameters than the RL-tuned LoRA (0.29M against 18.8M). We fit them to match the velocity predictions of public RL-tuned checkpoints, with no reward in the objective. Although the vectors are identical for every prompt, token, and sampling seed, they recover 90.0-96.3% of the reward gains of six RL-tuned image models, and 89.8% and 111.9% on two RL-tuned video models. Initialized at zero and trained with online RL, the vectors alone exceed the reward gains of weight-based RL on text rendering (OCR) and human preference (PickScore) on SD3.5-M. A constant shift thus suffices for most of the gain of policy-gradient RL post-training: without new input-dependent transformations, it steers the pretrained generator toward what it can already compute.

Title: Posterior Matching Distillation

  • Authors: Hidir Yesiltepe, Julius Berner, Arash Vahdat, Pinar Yanardag
  • Subjects: cs.CV
  • Abstract URL: https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26629
  • Pdf URL: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.26629
  • Copy Paste: [[2611.26629]] Posterior Matching Distillation(https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.26629)
  • Keywords: diffusion
  • Abstract: Diffusion distillation accelerates image and video generation by compressing a pretrained teacher's iterative sampling process into a few network evaluations. Distribution-based methods achieve this by matching the student's output distribution to the teacher's. This matching is often driven by reverse-KL objectives, whose mode-seeking bias favors dominant teacher modes at the expense of diversity across samples. We introduce Posterior Matching Distillation (PMD), a new member of the distribution-based distillation family that combines mode-covering posterior sampling with any-step generation. At zero population loss, PMD recovers the teacher's full posterior of clean images at each trained noise level. Its stochastic student supports any-step sampling budgets within the trained timestep grid without further training. To learn these posteriors, PMD matches exponentially reweighted averages of student samples conditioned on a fixed noisy state to corresponding teacher posterior means. Under Gaussian corruption, exponentially tilted posterior mean is a single teacher evaluation at a shifted input. We prove that, matching tilted means over an open neighborhood uniquely identifies the full posterior. PMD thus supervises the entire conditional distribution through a family of mean constraints, without any auxiliary student-score network. Experiments on SDXL and FLUX.2 [klein] 4B demonstrate that PMD achieves the best FID among 4-step methods on both models, while maintaining competitive or leading performance across prompt-alignment and perceptual-quality metrics.
